● Slice 1 accepted (c3093db, 9 tests, 542 lines). The frozen model:

  - ProposedCandidate { identity: tags::Candidate, witnesses: 
  NonEmpty<CandidateWitness> }, built through a new that rejects empty lists and
  duplicate WitnessIds.
  - TraceFact<T> = Known(T) | Deferred(DeferredFactReason), with Known(None)
  meaning proven absent and Deferred meaning not established — the distinction
  every later rejection depends on.
  - TraceSlotId/TraceStratumId/PartnerClassId/DeferredFeatureId are
  filter-contract identities, deliberately not aliases of grammar id spaces, so
  no pass can reject on evidence produced by ordinal coincidence.

● Found a real hole the agent's own defense misses. test_support is
  unconditionally pub in the shipped crate, and allow_list_filter takes
  caller-supplied Box<dyn CandidateFilterPass> — and CandidateFilterPass is a
  public trait. So any downstream caller can implement a pass that rejects
  everything, author a RejectionProof whose fields it copies straight off the
  witness (they're all public), allow-list its own triple, and run Enforce.
  Every structural check in that verifier is trivially satisfiable by the
  forger, because the forger authors both the pass and the proof.

  That publicly bypasses the contract's headline guarantee. The agent's claim
  that a caller "cannot widen a proof into one that admits an arbitrary claim"
  is wrong for exactly this reason.

  Three judgment calls I'm letting stand:

  - TraceFactKind instead of TraceFact — the plan's Task 4 snippet
  (DeferReason::MissingTraceFact(TraceFact::Slot)) simply doesn't compile
  against the frozen model; TraceFact is the generic Known/Deferred enum with no
  Slot variant. The agent's spelling is correct and I've told slice 3 not to
  re-litigate it.
  - Every witness is evaluated even after one survives — costs nothing today
  (the legacy adapter emits one witness per candidate) and buys
  order-independent evidence that slice 10's Off-vs-Enforce comparison needs.
  - candidates_rejected counts decisions, not removals, so Shadow reports both a
  rejection and a retention for the same candidate. That's what slice 9's
  shadow correlation will read.

  feature and required to prove the gate refuses by compiling a probe without it
  — a gate nobody watched refuse isn't a gate. Part B is the production
  verifier, where I've added a requirement the plan omitted: every forged-proof
  test needs a paired positive case, otherwise a verifier that rejects
  everything passes the entire forgery suite.

✻ Brewed for 2m 14s

❯ How are we building these filters - we ahve the pgpack distribution format -  
  how does this "filter" step fit into there?  Explain the chosen architecture  
  and how we can distribute these filters in the WASM environment?  Can we ship 
  just configurations that are parsed and run?  Do we have to ship              
  bingrammar?aries per                                                          

● Good question, and it's one the plan genuinely doesn't answer — the contract
  stops at the Rust API boundary. Let me get the facts rather than reason from
  the name.

  Searched for 2 patterns, read 4 files (ctrl+o to expand)

● The plan is silent on this — deliberately scoped to the Rust API boundary,
  ending at a generator handoff contract, not a distribution contract. So part
  of this is "here's what the architecture already implies" and part is a real
  gap. Let me separate those, because the facts are better than I expected.

  What the filter is made of

  The design splits into two pieces with very different distribution stories:

  Grammar-independent code — the pipeline (filter_into, any-witness-survives,
  budget bypass), the nine proof categories, and the RejectionProofVerifier.
  Compiled once into whatever runtime you ship. Not per-grammar, not
  per-language.

  Grammar-derived data — FilterIndex (slice 4): morpheme ownership, rule
  ownership, POS/MPR signatures, slot/stratum transitions. Plus a profile name
  (StructuralV1 | SymbolicV1 | BoundaryLocalV1) — a closed enum, so an
  identifier, not code.

  So: no binaries per grammar. Configuration + derived tables, parsed and run
  against one compiled pass library.

  Slice 7 makes that stronger than it sounds. The trace DFA is transitions: 
  Vec<Vec<StateId>> plus rejecting: BTreeMap<StateId, ProofTemplate> — a
  table-driven recognizer, not generated code. Even the "compiled" fast path is
  pure data. That was chosen for testability (exhaustive equivalence against the
  imperative pass), but it happens to be exactly the property that makes
  filters shippable.

  Where it lands in .pgpack

  Container v1 is magic | version | 3 length prefixes | manifest JSON | runtime 
  payload | foma payload | SHA-256. Two hooks already fit:

  - required_runtime_features.extensions — open, append-only string set, checked
  at load as required ⊆ provided, where provided is derived from the build and
  never read from the file. A pack stamping candidate-filter.structural.v1 is
  refused by a runtime that lacks it, with a typed error rather than a crash.
  - package_fingerprint — SHA-256 binding the payloads together so they can't be
  mixed across grammars. A FilterIndex shipped alongside must be bound by the
  same fingerprint, or a "verified proof" degrades into "consistent with
  whatever index happened to load."

  Containment is the wrong direction for one risk, though. required ⊆ provided
  stops an old runtime from loading a pack needing new features. It does not
  stop a newer runtime from enforcing BoundaryLocalV1 on a pack certified only
  for StructuralV1 — the pack requires less, so containment passes happily.
  Since slice 10's whole promotion ladder is per-grammar and evidence-gated, the
  manifest needs a positive statement of which profiles are certified for this 
  grammar, and the runtime must enforce the intersection, not its own maximum.
  That's a genuine design gap, not something the existing mechanism covers.

  The blocker you should know about

  WASM can't run this yet, and not because of the filter. pg-wasm/src/pack.rs is
  a load-time envelope gate only — it validates the manifest, reports trust and
  health, and explicitly does not construct an analyzer. The reason is in
  pg-pack/src/format.rs:

  ▎ pg_grammar::model::Grammar is not serde-serializable today … the [runtime 
  ▎ payload] has no real producer yet anywhere in this workspace — its tests use
  ▎ only synthetic byte fixtures, honestly, because that is all that exists.

  The foma payload has a real producer (fsm_write_binary). The HC runtime
  payload does not. So today WASM ships a proposer it can't confirm with — and
  FilterIndex is derived from exactly the grammar facts that would ride in that
  unserialized payload. Making the filter distributable is downstream of
  make-wasm-analysis-only (the openspec change that exists for this), not of
  anything in the current eleven slices.

  Worth noting the filter helps most in that environment — single-threaded,
  tight memory, where cheap symbolic rejection before expensive derivational
  search matters more than it does on a 20-core box.

  What I'd change now, cheaply

  Two constraints on the in-flight work so we don't have to retrofit:

  1. FilterIndex must own its data, not borrow the grammar. The plan says it
  "owns immutable arrays/maps" — good — but also that it reuses
  MorphotacticIndex as the transition authority. If slice 4 holds a
  &MorphotacticIndex, the index stops being serializable and the whole
  data-not-binaries story needs surgery later. I'll require owned facts plus an
  explicit "this is derived from, and fingerprint-bound to, one grammar" shape.
  2. Stable IDs are already wire-ready — StablePassId(&'static str),
  StableRuleId { family, ordinal }, alphabet_version: u16. Nothing to do; just
  don't let a later slice replace them with array indices.

  I'd leave the actual pgpack section, profile stamping, and the
  certified-profile intersection rule as a separate change after slice 11,
  because it depends on decisions (runtime payload serialization) that this
  program doesn't own.

  Want me to fold constraint 1 into slice 4's brief now — it's the one that gets
  expensive to undo — and open an openspec change for the distribution half?

  as needing a check — whether the grammar model's shape is actually stable —

● Concrete answer on the precondition you flagged:

  The grammar model is empirically stable. Over 90 days, 10 commits touch
  pg-grammar/src/model.rs, and most are cosmetic — the hc-*→pg-* rename, a
  comment sweep, doc-link fixes. Only four are substantive, each 6–15 net lines,
  one adding a single top-level item, none removing any. Your "it shouldn't
  change" holds up.

  But churn rate isn't the risk. The model has 16 enums — PhonRuleDef,
  MorphRuleDef, PatternNode, MprGroupMatchType, AllomorphOwner, and eleven more
  — and none is #[non_exhaustive]. The dangerous change is one added variant,
  which is a one-line commit that compiles cleanly everywhere a filter pass
  wrote _ =>. That's the silent-unsoundness path: a pass whose wildcard arm
  means "not applicable, keep going" starts treating a brand-new construct as
  harmless and discards a valid parse. HC-rust would likely break loudly on the
  same change; the filter would not.

  So the gate almost writes itself, and it's cheaper than a shape digest: no 
  filter pass may wildcard-match a grammar-model enum — either it enumerates
  every variant, or its wildcard arm returns Defer, never Keep. That converts
  "someone added a variant" from a silent soundness bug into either a compile
  error or a harmless loss of filtering. It's mechanically checkable and needs
  no versioning machinery at all.

  I'm feeding that to the challenger as a specific claim to attack rather than
  letting it emerge as a suggestion.

  Recorded decisions, all four:

  ┌───────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │               │                        Decision                         │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Soundness     │ By construction, conditional on grammar-model shape     │
  │               │ stability                                               │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Pack config   │ None in v1 — runtime decides, nothing written to        │
  │               │ .pgpack                                                 │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Budget        │ Finish derivation, cache in the local WASM/desktop      │
  │ overrun       │ store                                                   │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ First release │ Enforce from day one                                    │
  └───────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Two risks I'd flag before the challenger gets there. The cache reintroduces 
  exactly the staleness problem that deriving-from-grammar was chosen to delete
  — a cached index is derived data that can outlive its input, so its key has to
  cover both grammar identity and filter code version, or a stale hit silently
  enforces one grammar's facts against another. And enforce-from-day-one plus no
  customer oracle leaves no diagnosis path: a user whose parse goes missing
  can't isolate the filter as the cause. You didn't ask for a kill switch and
  I'm not overturning that, but I've asked the planner to price one rather than
  pass over it silently.

✻ Cogitated for 5m 49s

❯ Obviously a cache should be invalidated from an updated grammar or an updated 
  pangloss runtime.                                                             

● Agreed, and both keys already exist or are cheap:

  - Grammar — package_fingerprint in the pack manifest, already a SHA-256 over
  both framed payloads, so it changes whenever the grammar does.
  - Runtime — crate semver, which pg-wasm already reads from CARGO_PKG_VERSION_*
  for hc_port_semver.

  The one trap worth naming: semver doesn't move between dev builds, and this
  team is about to iterate on filter derivation daily. A rebuilt binary with
  changed derivation logic and an unchanged version string gets a stale cache
  hit that silently enforces yesterday's facts — and it'd hit you in
  development, where you're least likely to suspect the cache. Cheap fix with no
  machinery: don't cache in debug builds at all, and add an explicit
  schema-version constant alongside the semver for release builds. Caching only
  buys anything for a long-lived deployed runtime anyway.

● Plan is 939 lines, well structured — and notably §8.1 is titled "Whether it
  earns its place," so the planner did question the cache rather than just

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

● Read §8 myself. The plan is more honest than I expected on the part I flagged
  as riskiest, and it caught two things I'd have missed:

  - Native has no grammar identity to key on. pangloss pack only writes packs;
  nothing native reads one back. So package_fingerprint doesn't exist on the
  native path, and the plan keys native on a SHA-256 of the raw grammar-source
  bytes taken before pg_grammar::load parses them — conservative by
  construction, since a grammar differing only in a comment the loader ignores
  hashes differently and simply re-derives rather than falsely hitting.
  - env! resolves against the compiling crate, so reading CARGO_PKG_VERSION_*
  inside pg-foma yields pg-foma's version, not pg-wasm's.

  Final key is (grammar_identity, pg_foma_semver, FILTER_INDEX_SOURCE_HASH, 
  FILTER_INDEX_SCHEMA_VERSION), where the source hash comes from a build.rs
  hashing only the files FilterIndex::derive actually depends on — deliberately
  not the whole candidate_filter tree, so iterating on a pass doesn't force
  spurious misses. That's mechanical rather than discipline-based, which is the
  right trade.

  Two things I'd highlight in its favor: it states plainly that the key is not
  airtight — the build-script file list is a human-maintained boundary and it
  says so instead of implying otherwise — and it recommends specifying the cache
  fully but not building the persistent store until measurement shows a grammar
  whose cold derivation cost actually justifies it. Given you asked for
  caching, it presents that as evidence for you rather than removing it
  unilaterally.

  The residual risk I'd still put on you: that build.rs file list is the one
  place a mistake reintroduces stale hits, and it's maintained by hand.

  Challenger is running against all of it now. I'll hold judgment on the rest of
  the plan until it reports, rather than pre-empting the attack I asked for.

● Slice 3 accepted — a7e46f5 (seam gate) + a8fb35e (verifier), 30 + 12 tests
  green, tree clean.

  The forgery coverage is the real deliverable: 9 categories × 9 generic
  corruptions = 81, plus 25 payload corruptions and 8 deferred-fact cases. And
  it ruled out the failure mode I was most worried about — a verifier that
  refuses everything would pass every forgery test — three independent ways, the
  strongest being a permanent test that runs all 25 payload forgeries through a
  claim-blind verifier and requires each to kill there while being retained by
  the production verifier. That measures what the claim re-derivation is
  actually worth instead of assuming it. It reached that after trying a
  temporary if true { return Ok(()) } bypass, getting blocked by the permission
  classifier, and concluding the permanent test was the better answer. It is.

● Challenger found something that matters more than the rest. Verifying its

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

● Both claims verified. The central one is real and it's the crux of the whole
  design.

  check_claim(witness, proof) takes no grammar access at all — and FilterContext
  carries only identity, candidate_ordinal, mode. So for categories like
  ForbiddenTransition, the verifier confirms the trace really has slots A and B,
  but nothing confirms the grammar forbids A→B. A pass or a FilterIndex that
  wrongly believes a transition is forbidden produces a proof that verifies
  perfectly and kills a valid candidate.

  That's precisely the failure the proof-carrying architecture exists to
  prevent, and it's fatal to "sound by construction" as the license to ship to
  grammars nobody has tested. Without grammar re-derivation, the verifier
  catches proof forgery and trace inconsistency — not wrongness. "By
  construction" quietly reduces to "the pass is correct," which is the thing we
  wanted evidence for.

  It also undercuts the cache story. The plan argues a bad cached index is "just
  a performance issue"; it isn't, because nothing downstream would catch it.

  There's a clean fix, and it makes the cache claim true. Give the verifier 
  &Grammar and have it re-derive the fact from the grammar directly — not from 
  the FilterIndex the pass used. Two independent readings of the same
  authoritative source catch pass logic bugs, and re-deriving from the grammar
  rather than the derived index also catches a wrong or stale derivation. A
  corrupted cache then produces rejections whose proofs fail verification →
  Defer → candidate retained. The cache genuinely becomes a performance-only
  concern.

  Cost: FilterContext carries grammar access, and each rejection pays a second
  fact lookup — on the path where we're already skipping an expensive HC
  confirmation, so the trade is good.

  The challenger's other findings, all verified or credible:

  - make_report.rs:655 calls pg_pack::read_pack in production — the plan's "no
  other caller" claim is false. Doesn't change its conclusion, but it was stated
  as verified.
  - The no-wildcard gate's mechanical check tests the wrong property — Task A
  asserts zero #[non_exhaustive] in model.rs, which is not the same as "every
  match site is exhaustive or defers," and it doesn't reach pg-rules helpers
  like morpheme_co_occurrence_rules_ok that slice 5 plans to call. This is
  exactly why I held slice 4.
  - Tasks D and E are scheduled before Task F's measurement despite §8 saying
  the store should be gated behind it.
  - Verdict: SALVAGEABLE, not sound.

● User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · Should the proof verifier get grammar access so it can independently 
     re-derive the grammar fact behind each rejection? → I don't know what this 
     is for.  the verifier - is it really needed as a production system?  Would 
     it merely be a test harness or some research proof that is in markdown 
     somewhere and referenced in code?

● Fair challenge, and it exposes that my &Grammar recommendation was solving the
  wrong problem. Here's the honest accounting.

  What the verifier actually catches today. I checked: it validates the envelope
  (identity, witness id, grammar/lexicon revision, lexical origin, cited units)
  and then re-derives the claim against the witness's own trace — that unit 3
  really does carry slot A, that the allomorph set really was exhausted. So it
  catches exactly one bug class: a pass whose written claim contradicts the 
  trace it was looking at.

  What it does not catch: a pass whose grammar reasoning is wrong. If a pass
  wrongly believes slot A→B is forbidden, it emits a claim that is perfectly
  consistent with the trace, and the verifier passes it. That's the dominant
  risk, and it's untouched.

  And there is no adversary. Every pass is first-party compiled code in the same
  crate. Nothing untrusted authors a proof. So in production the verifier is
  our code checking our code against data our code just produced three lines
  earlier — that's an assertion, not verification. The whole forgery threat
  model that justifies 9 categories of payload is a test threat model.

  So: you're right, it isn't a production system. Where independent
  re-derivation genuinely belongs is the place your plan already puts it — the
  model-check tasks in slices 4/5/6, which compare each pass against an
  exhaustive reference predicate over small enumerated domains. That's where a
  second implementation is affordable, exhaustive rather than sampled, and
  actually independent. Test-time, not per-rejection.

  But the proof payload does earn its place in production — just not as
  something to verify. It's the explanation: the death ledger's "why did this
  candidate die," slice 9's shadow correlation, and a user's only diagnosis path
  under enforce-from-day-one. That's data you want carried, not checked.

  What I'd do, which is a net deletion:

  ┌──────────────────────────┬─────────────────────┬───────────────────────┐
  │                          │     Production      │     Test / shadow     │
  ├──────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ Proof payload            │ carried as the      │ same                  │
  │                          │ diagnostic          │                       │
  ├──────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ Envelope check           │ keep — near-free,   │                       │
  │ (identity, witness,      │ catches mis-wiring  │ same                  │
  │ revisions)               │                     │                       │
  ├──────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ Claim re-derivation      │ drop from the hot   │ full, plus exhaustive │
  │                          │ path                │  reference predicates │
  └──────────────────────────┴─────────────────────┴───────────────────────┘

  That removes per-rejection cost, deletes the argument for giving the verifier
  grammar access, and leaves the safety story resting on the things that
  actually carry it: passes that defer on any uncertainty, the no-wildcard rule,
  the panic guard, fail-open everywhere.

  Two things this does not let us drop. Slice 3's 81 forgery tests stay valuable
  — they prove the test-time oracle is strict enough to be worth gating on. And
  it makes a kill switch more attractive, not less, since production loses a
  runtime backstop it never really had.

  Worth being clear about the cost: this re-scopes work slice 3 just landed. The
  code isn't wasted — RejectionProofVerifier becomes the test oracle — but
  filter_then_confirm in slice 9 would wire the cheap path, not the full one.

  Want me to re-scope it that way, or keep full verification in production and
  accept the cost for defense-in-depth?

● Rescoped and in flight.

  Contract updated first (34bb3f7), because the reasoning needs to outlive this
  conversation. It now states plainly that Full checking is not a production
  safety mechanism, why — no untrusted party authors a proof, so the forgery
  threat model is a testing model — and where independent re-derivation actually
  lives: the model tests, which are a second implementation over exhaustive
  domains rather than the same code checking itself.

  Design: ProofCheckDepth { Envelope, Full }, deliberately orthogonal to
  FilterMode rather than derived from it, so Enforce + Full stays available as a
  debugging lever. Production defaults to Envelope; tests and Shadow default to
  Full.

  The requirement I care most about is the third test: a payload forgery that 
  Full refuses must be accepted at Envelope. That deliberately pins the weaker
  guarantee as a passing assertion rather than leaving it implied by absence —
  otherwise someone reads "verified" a year from now and assumes more than we
  deliver. Nothing is deleted; RejectionProofVerifier becomes the test and
  shadow instrument.

● Done — ProofCheckDepth { Off, Full }, agent redirected, contract updated
  (adfa11a).

  Your two bullets are right, with one refinement on the first that matters for
  blame assignment. There are really two different comparisons, and only one of
  them can convict the filter:

  - Off vs Enforce, same proposer, filter as the only variable. A difference
  here is the filter's fault, necessarily. This is slice 10's gate, on both
  deduplicated AnalysisIdentity sets and exact WordAnalysis multisets.
  - Full HC vs the FST path, which is what you described. This tests proposer
  and filter together, so a failure can't be blamed on the filter without the
  first comparison to disambiguate. Still worth having — it's the existing
  parity machinery, and it catches proposer gaps the filter can't — but it's a
  different question.

  So the debugging order on a failure is: does it parse with filters off? If
  yes, filter. If no, proposer.

  One consequence you may not have intended. Once runtime checking is gone,
  reproducibility carries the entire safety story — "we can re-derive it from
  the diagnostic" only works if the same inputs always produce the same
  decisions. So determinism stops being a nice property and becomes a tested
  requirement; I've pinned it in the contract and added a test to the slice.

  And it promotes the kill switch you declined earlier. Not as insurance — as
  the primary diagnostic instrument. Under the design as it now stands, the
  first question about a word that stopped parsing is "does it parse with
  filtering off," and that single answer isolates the filter from the proposer
  and from HC. Without the switch, a user's only path is to send you a grammar
  and wait. I've written that into the contract as reasoning rather than as a
  decision, since it's your call — but the argument for it is materially
  stronger than when you turned it down, because it's no longer redundant with a
  runtime check that no longer exists.

  Net deletion from where we started: no per-rejection claim re-derivation, no
  envelope tier, no &Grammar on the verifier, no FilterContext change to carry
  it. What production keeps is a proof record and deterministic counters.

✻ Crunched for 1m 58s

❯ You keep assuming that the filters will have errors that the users will know  
  about and report on.  This is a spell checker - it "just works" or "doesn't   
  work".  We need to iron all this out before deployment in testing.            

● You're right, and I've been repeating that assumption for three messages.
  There's no feedback loop — a spell checker that silently drops a word produces
  no bug report, just a slightly worse tool. So production diagnostics have
  almost no value, and the kill-switch-as-diagnostic argument I just made
  collapses with them. FilterMode::Off already exists for the harness; nothing
  new needs building. That's another deletion.

  The real consequence is that the entire safety burden moves to pre-deployment,
  and the bar for those gates has to rise to match, because nothing downstream
  will ever catch what they miss.

  That lands hardest on slice 8. The plan already refuses to certify on partial
  evidence — an incomplete or timeout-censored oracle is recorded NotDetermined
  and fails, explicitly — but it also documents what's actually available today:

  - Indonesian: a 121-word parity run
  - Sena: sampled, not complete
  - Amharic: excludes timed-out rows
  - Aweti: bounded and partial
  - Mbugwe: no committed word-parity harness at all

  Under the old design that was one gate among several. Under no-field-feedback
  it's the whole thing — and by its own fail-closed rule, zero of the five would
  certify today. That's the honest status, and it's the thing to fix before
  anything ships, not the filter code.

  What substitutes for field feedback is completeness, and there are two kinds
  available:

  - Exhaustive model checks over small enumerated domains (slices 4–6). These
  are complete rather than sampled — every trace up to length 4, every subset of
  3 allomorphs, every truth assignment. A pass that survives those is proven on
  its whole domain, not spot-checked. This is the strongest evidence in the
  program and it's cheap.
  - Complete, uncensored oracle runs on all five corpora. Every word, no
  sampling, no timeout exclusions — a timeout is a non-answer, and treating it
  as a pass is exactly how a filter bug would hide.

  The second is the expensive one and it's where the work is: full word lists
  rather than samples, and resolving the Amharic timeouts and the missing Mbugwe
  harness rather than excluding them.

  So before slice 4 goes out, one thing to settle: is "all five corpora,
  complete and uncensored, zero oracle-positive kills, plus exhaustive model
  checks per pass" the deployment bar? If so, the Amharic timeout resolution and
  a Mbugwe harness become prerequisites for shipping and probably want to start
  in parallel — they're likely slower than the filter work itself. If the bar
  is lower, I'd rather know now than build the gates twice.

● Yes — and it's better than where I was heading, mainly because it's synthetic
  and committed, so the primary gate runs in CI with no private-corpus
  dependency at all.

  Three things worth adding, one of which is a correction to your framing.

  For the filter specifically, the strongest oracle isn't a golden file — it's 
  HC-rust live in the same binary. Your loop is "run HC-rust, then
  FST-filter-HC-rust, compare." Slice 10 already does exactly that as Off vs
  Enforce, in one process, same grammar, same build. That's stronger than
  comparing against a transcribed golden set because there's no transcription
  step and nothing to go stale. The filter's obligation is precisely HC(F(P(w)))
  == HC(P(w)), and both sides are computable on the spot.

  The C# golden sets answer a different question — whether HC-rust is right at
  all. Essential, but a port-correctness question, not a filter question. If
  HC-rust and the golden set disagree, the filter is innocent.

  A caveat I hit while checking. The staged fixtures aren't uniformly
  C#-derived. One says so in its own header:

  ▎ Oracle: pangloss (pg_parse::Morpher, driven directly) — NOT the C# founding 
  ▎ oracle — authored and verified against this repo's own Rust engine only. 
  ▎ Signatures below are transcribed verbatim from that run, not hand-derived.

  So parts of conformance-staging/ are self-oracled against Rust. That's fine
  for filter validation — pinning HC-rust's own behavior is exactly what the
  filter must not perturb — but it means those fixtures can't serve as ground
  truth for the port. The 25 upstream machine/conformance fixtures are the
  C#-founded ones. Worth knowing which set you're leaning on for which claim.

  The blind spot in the loop as stated: "run both, look at misses" passes
  perfectly for a filter that rejects nothing. Zero misses and zero value are
  indistinguishable by that comparison alone. Slice 10 already pairs it with a
  fire count — nonzero verified rejections, strictly fewer candidates reaching
  HC — and that pairing has to survive; a green run with zero firing is a
  non-result, not a pass.

  Given all that, I'd reframe slice 8: demote the five private corpora from 
  primary gate to supplementary, and make Off-vs-Enforce over the conformance
  fixtures the primary gate. That deletes the corpus-availability dependency
  from the critical path, kills the privacy surface, and makes the main gate
  reproducible by anyone. The private corpora still earn a place — real grammars
  hit construct combinations synthetic ones don't — but as a secondary check
  whose absence doesn't block, rather than a fail-closed prerequisite nobody can
  currently satisfy.

  The one dependency that framing exposes: the conformance grammars carrying
  "the full load" is the other worktree's work, so the filter's deployment
  readiness is gated on their coverage, not on filter code. Worth knowing whose
  critical path that actually is.

  Want me to rewrite slice 8 along those lines before slice 4 goes out?

● Before the merge mechanics — the premise needs correcting, because this isn't
  the conformance-grammar work.

  What's actually there. Branch docs/hc-semantic-catalog, 15 commits, clean,
  4,992 lines across 11 files. But the files are
  Conformance/SemanticCoverage/DtdInventoryReader.cs, CSharpInventoryReader.cs
  (Roslyn), HermitCrab/SemanticBranch.cs, and a coverage-catalog doc. That's a
  source-analysis tool that inventories what HC semantically does — DTD
  surfaces, C# types, semantic operations. It's the instrument that tells you
  which conformance grammars you still need. It does not produce grammars or
  expected analyses.

  And you can't merge it. It's a checkout of sillsdev/machine — remote
  github.com/sillsdev/machine.git, based on origin/conformance-framework, no
  main in that repo. PanGloss consumes it only as a submodule pinned at caa4ddd.
  So there is no branch merge available; the only mechanism is:

  1. Verify the claimed 61/61 dotnet gate — the previous agent explicitly did
  not run it, so that number is unverified.
  2. Land the commits on conformance-framework upstream.
  3. In PanGloss: git -C machine checkout <new sha>, commit the gitlink, done.

  That's a one-line change to PanGloss whenever it's ready. Nothing about the
  filter work needs to wait for it.

  Its state argues against blocking too. The plan there has Tasks 0, 1, 2A done
  and 2B–10 open — 50 steps. Task 2B is explicitly blocked on "the other agent's
  backend fixes and the Task 3 audit seam," and Codex stopped because its tool
  allowance is exhausted until August 18. Serializing the filter program behind
  that is weeks of idle time for a dependency that answers a different question.

  What you actually asked for is buildable today. "Verification for each filter
  type — assert that specific traces are removed by a specific filter, each one
  holds its own weight" needs purpose-built fixtures per pass: a grammar where
  the ownership pass fires and nothing else does, one where the transition pass
  fires, and so on. That's authored grammars plus expected analyses, and this
  repo already has the machinery — .claude/skills/conformance-grammars, 25
  staged fixtures in conformance-staging/, and the established pattern of a
  pathology-mimic grammar per construct. No C# catalog required.

  The catalog answers "have we covered every HC construct anywhere?" That's a
  global completeness audit, and it's genuinely valuable — but it's a later gate
  over the whole fixture corpus, not a prerequisite for proving one filter
  earns its weight.

  So what I'd propose:

  - Don't block. Keep the submodule at caa4ddd. Bump the pin when the C# work
  lands, as a standalone one-line change.
  - Add a slice before slice 4: author one staged conformance fixture per
  planned pass, each with a grammar that triggers exactly that pass, plus the
  assertion that the pass fires a specific nonzero number of times and that Off
  and Enforce produce identical analyses. That is precisely "each filter holds
  its own weight," and it makes every later pass slice testable the moment it's
  written.
  - When the catalog lands, run it as a coverage audit over the fixture corpus
  to find constructs no fixture exercises — the thing it's actually good at.

  The one real cost of not waiting: fixtures we author now are guided by our own
  judgment about which HC constructs matter, and the catalog might later reveal
  a construct we never thought to cover. That's a gap the catalog closes by 
  adding fixtures, not by invalidating existing ones.

  Want me to write that per-filter fixture slice and slot it in ahead of slice
  4?

● Written in as Task 3c and committed (dc7a42b), sequenced 3b → 3c → 4.

  The shape, and the one design decision worth your eye:

  Fixtures are authorable now, before any pass exists. A fixture pins HC-rust's
  own analyses for a construct — which is precisely the reference a pass must
  not perturb. So the slow, judgment-heavy half (authoring a synthetic grammar
  that provokes one pass and as few others as possible) front-loads, and each
  later pass slice lands against a target already waiting.

  Nine directories under conformance-staging/filter-passes/. Eight are
  authorable. partner-pairing gets an expectation file marked not-yet-provokable
  with the reason recorded as data: the grammar model compiles circumfix halves
  into a single cross-product allomorph, so no authored grammar can produce the
  partner events that pass consumes. Better as a machine-readable declaration
  than a comment someone later mistakes for an oversight.

  The mechanism that makes this real is the anti-rot gate. A fixture with no
  assertion is worse than no fixture — it reads as coverage while proving
  nothing. So each carries { pass_id, min_fire_count, status }, and the harness
  fails loudly on three conditions: a fixture still awaiting-pass whose pass now
  exists in the registry, a fixture naming an unknown pass, and a registered
  pass with no fixture at all. That last one is what stops a pass from quietly
  shipping without evidence.

  Right now every fixture is awaiting-pass and no fire count is asserted. The
  gate reports that state explicitly rather than showing a green that means
  nothing — which is the same standard I've been holding the slices to.

  Two things I did not do, deliberately:

  - The submodule pin stays at caa4ddd. The C# semantic-catalog work is a
  different repository and 50 open steps blocked until August 18; bumping the
  pin is a one-line change whenever it lands.
  - Expected analyses come from pg_parse::Morpher, transcribed verbatim — same
  convention the existing staged fixtures document, including their honesty that
  the oracle is this repo's Rust engine and not the C# founding oracle. A
  hand-derived signature is a guess with a test wrapped around it.

  Slice 3b is still running in the worktree (I can see its in-progress edits to
  pipeline.rs, test_support.rs, and both test files). I staged only the plan
  file. Once it reports and I've verified it, 3c goes out.

● Agent "Rescope proof checking depth" finished · 43m 39s

● Slice 3b landed. One of its disclosed assumptions is a real question, and the
  answer depends on where the ledger gets its pass identity — checking that.

  Read 1 file, ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Confirmed, and it's a real defect. PassEvent.pass_id comes from pass.id() (the
  pipeline's own knowledge, line 492), but WitnessDeath.pass_id comes from 
  proof.pass_id (line 502) — and at Off nothing checks the two agree.

  WitnessDeath is the production diagnostic. So a pass that stamps its proof
  with another pass's ID produces a death record blaming the wrong filter, and
  since re-deriving offline now starts from that record, the investigation
  starts in the wrong place. The fix is better than restoring the guard the
  agent removed: take the ledger's identity from pass.id() too, making
  mislabeling impossible by construction rather than merely detected.

● Agent(Fix death record pass attribution)

● Slice 3b accepted (ae722d2) — 31 + 16 tests green, plain build succeeds.

  ProofCheckDepth { Off, Full } with for_mode mapping Shadow → Full and
  everything else to Off. Depth is threaded as a parameter rather than hung on
  FilterContext, deliberately, so a pass cannot see or branch on it — a pass
  that behaved differently when checked would defeat the point.

  The test pinning the reduced guarantee is stronger than I asked for. I
  specified payload forgeries; it covers all 106 (25 payload + 81 generic), and
  each iteration asserts the same forgery is refused at Full and kills a real
  witness at Off. It cannot pass unless depth genuinely changes behavior. The
  assertion message spells out that production takes a rejection at face value
  "however corrupt its proof is" — which is exactly the fact that should be hard
  to miss in a year.

  Three disclosures worth surfacing:

  - 23 dead-code warnings in a plain build — RejectionProofVerifier and its
  helpers now reach production through nothing, since their only entry point
  sits behind the test-support feature. The agent argued from the call graph
  that these pre-date its change rather than measuring a baseline, and said so
  rather than implying it had measured. That's correct reasoning and honestly
  labeled. It also declined to paper over them with #[allow(dead_code)], and
  disclosed the one #[allow] it did add.
  - Two existing contract tests changed meaning. The allow-list seam no longer
  constrains anything at the production default, so those tests now request Full
  explicitly. Assertions unchanged — but the behavior they describe no longer
  exists in production, which is the intended consequence of your decision,
  stated plainly.
  - The determinism test found no nondeterminism across identical runs — same
  retained identities, same PassEvent vectors, same deaths, same counters.
  That's the property the whole offline-re-derivation story depends on, now
  pinned.

  The attribution fix is running. Once it lands, slice 3c (the per-pass
  fixtures) goes out, then slice 4 — which by then carries three requirements it
  didn't start with: the panic guard, the no-wildcard-over-grammar-enums rule,
  and an owned (not borrowed) FilterIndex.

❯ Remove the dead code- or if is needed for test, move it there.                

● Those 23 are all one cluster: RejectionProofVerifier, its check_*/established*
  helpers in proof.rs, CandidateFilter::{new, verifying}, and
  admissible_catalog — reachable only through test_support::verified_filter,
  which is already feature-gated. So it's test-only code sitting in production
  modules.

  Gating it behind test-support works, but it leaves a wart: CandidateFilter
  would carry a verifier field that exists only under a feature, and the Full
  arm of admit would have to be cfg'd out too. That messiness is a signal.

  The alternative is to notice that if production never checks, the pipeline 
  doesn't need a verifier at all. Verification becomes post-hoc: run the filter
  with a ledger, then assert every proof it recorded verifies. That's a stronger
  test than the current one — "no bad proof was emitted" beats "the pipeline
  declined to act on a bad proof" — and it deletes ProofCheckDepth, the verifier
  field, the admit branch, and PassOutcome::ProofRejected along with the dead
  code.

  Cost: it discards ProofCheckDepth, which landed twenty minutes ago.

● User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · How far should the dead-code cleanup go? → Post-hoc verification 
     (recommended)

  attribution fix to land first, since both touch pipeline.rs.

● Agent(Post-hoc verification refactor)

● Post-hoc refactor dispatched. Contract updated first (eb56257), so the agent
  implements against the committed decision rather than my description of it.

  Success criterion is blunt and checkable: a plain build with zero warnings. No
  #[allow(dead_code)] permitted — if something can't be removed, it gets named
  and justified.

  The requirement I made unmissable is the one the attribution fix exposed.
  WitnessDeath.pass_id is now trustworthy by construction, but rule_id and
  category still come straight off the proof, and with the inline verifier gone,
  post-hoc checking against admissible_proofs() is the only thing standing 
  between a pass and a death record stamped with a rule family it never 
  declared. Two of the three fields offline re-derivation starts from are
  otherwise unguarded anywhere. I also asked it to evaluate — not build —
  whether an undeclared (rule, category) could be made unconstructable at the
  type level, since prevention beats detection here.

  Three ledger-integrity residuals that agent surfaced are recorded against
  slice 11 rather than fixed now, because none of them can lose a valid parse —
  they only degrade the diagnostic:

  - BoundedDeathLedger caps events and deaths independently, so a stored death
  can cite an event ordinal the same ledger dropped.
  - After ordinal saturation every event carries u64::MAX, so post-saturation
  deaths share a non-unique key.
  - pass_ordinal saturates at u16::MAX, and WitnessDeath carries no
  pass_ordinal, so nothing cross-checks the two.

  Queue from here: 3d (running) → 3c (per-pass fixtures) → 4 (FilterIndex +
  structural passes, now carrying the panic guard, the no-wildcard rule, and the
  owned-index requirement).

● All anchors verified real, and two discrepancies worth catching before they
  bite:
● Both slices dispatched and running in parallel. While waiting I verified the
  plan's claims about existing code, since an agent wrote that plan and the
  challenger already caught one false "verified" claim in its sibling document.
  All anchors are real — resolve_pins (confirm.rs:132, already a pure function,
  so making it pub(crate) is trivial), MorphotacticIndex with next_state as the
  transition authority, confirm_batch_with_diagnostics. But two things the plan
  gets wrong:

  Slice 9 is under-specified in a way that would silently lose state. The plan
  names from_cached and into_parts. composite.rs actually has four: from_cached
  (925), from_cached_with_morpher (953), into_parts (982),
  into_parts_with_morpher (996). Carrying filter index/profile/revision through
  only the two named ones means the _with_morpher paths reset it silently —
  exactly the kind of thing that passes every test aimed at the documented entry
  points.

  Slice 5's "new pure function" already exists. morpheme_co_occurrence_rules_ok
  is at validity.rs:353, private, next to co_occurrence_rule_ok (337) and
  morpheme_co_occurrence_ok (370). The task is to expose and pin an existing
  function, not author one — and the distinction matters, because an agent told
  to "write a new pure function" will likely write a second implementation next
  to the first rather than notice the duplication.

  Both recorded against their tasks so the corrections travel with the work.

● All three confirmed:

  - verify_all iterates records, but nothing ever passes more than one.
  refusal() structurally extracts a single error, so every call site passes 0 or
  1 record. Mutating the loop to .take(1) would pass all ~150 tests — and a
  corpus-scale verify_recorded would then check one proof and report that
  everything re-derives.
  - recorded_rejections reads ledger.events() and never consults 
  omitted_events() or is_summary_only(). Over a capped ledger it silently
  verifies a prefix and returns Ok(()). Both of these only bite at scale, which
  is exactly when they'd be trusted most.
  - root_position_is_possible has an untested -1 branch. Delete
  identity.root_index == -1 || and the suite still passes — after which a
  MalformedIdentity proof against a legitimate rootless candidate verifies,
  blessing a pass that deletes every rootless analysis.

  The headline is good news, though, and it's the part that matters most: the
  reviewer traced every return, ?, early exit, and arithmetic op in the
  production filtering path and found no path that loses a valid candidate. The
  only removal is under Enforce when every witness rejected. Budget exhaustion,
  ordinal overflow, Defer, Off, Shadow, empty pass list, and mid-candidate stop
  all retain. Zero unwrap/expect/panic!/unchecked arithmetic in the production
  module.

  So every defect is in the evidence layer — which after the post-hoc refactor
  is the entire safety argument. That's the right place to be strict.

  coverage "9 categories × 9 corruptions = 81." The reviewer's deflation is fair

❯ For instance, for circumfix or matching phonology, the FST should be able to  
  say "this is the Left hand side of circumfix A with phonology 2 and phonology

● Yes, and the example is valid — for a stronger reason than the one you gave.
  Let me check one thing that bears directly on it.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Valid — and the justification is stronger than "easy to check."

  Why it's right: the reason to check afterwards rather than compose the
  constraint into the FST is state explosion. Enforcing circumfix agreement
  inside the automaton multiplies states by the (class × variant) combinations
  across the entire span between the two halves. That's the cross-product blowup
  this branch is named after, and it's what the contract means when it says the
  model "compiles the cross-product into one affix allomorph." Emitting labels
  and checking after keeps the FST small and makes the check O(n) over the
  trace. That's the canonical reason a filter beats an automaton constraint, and
  it's a much better argument than convenience.

  One refinement makes your "needs to know nothing of the grammar" claim 
  literally true. As stated, left emits "phonology 2" and right emits "phonology
  3" — and the filter must then know whether 2 and 3 are compatible. That's
  grammar knowledge, and it drags a lookup table back in. Instead, have the
  generator emit a canonical agreement key: both halves emit the same opaque
  token when they're compatible. The check collapses to key_left == key_right,
  with no table and no FilterIndex. Same information, but the compatibility
  decision is made where the grammar is already in hand.

  Slice 1 already built the carrier — LocalEvent::PartnerOpen(PartnerClassId) /
  PartnerClose(PartnerClassId) are in the model. So the trace side is ready; a
  stack keyed by class, matching on the agreement key, is a few dozen lines.

  What blocks it is upstream, not the filter. The grammar model collapses
  circumfix halves into a single cross-product allomorph before the FST is
  built, so the information is destroyed before anything could emit it. That's
  generator and model work — which is exactly why the contract keeps partner
  pairing out of every enforced profile, and why slice 3c is marking that
  fixture not-yet-provokable.

  The one thing worth checking before building it: foma's standard answer to
  non-local agreement is flag diacritics, which enforce exactly this class of
  constraint during traversal without the state explosion — that is their entire
  purpose. There's flag material already in gate.rs and precision.rs. So there
  are two explosion-free options, not one: flags enforce it in the FST and never
  propose the bad candidate; your design proposes it and rejects it cheaply.
  Which wins is empirical — flags cost traversal nondeterminism, filtering costs
  a proposal that dies. Worth deciding on evidence rather than by construction.

  I'm feeding this to the architecture reviewer now, since it's mid-flight on
  exactly this question.

● Stopping to report this rather than continuing — it's the most consequential
  thing found in this session, and it's about whether the program should

  All three load-bearing claims verified in code.

  1. The ownership pass targets candidates that already cost nothing.
  confirm_batch_impl resolves pins first and continues past every None before
  any parse begins (confirm.rs:305-315). A candidate that fails pin resolution
  never reaches HC's expensive path today. Filtering it earlier saves the cost
  of a continue.

  2. The transition pass re-applies an authority already applied.
  ExploreMode::Pruned is documented in the source as "the production default,"
  and it consults MorphotacticIndex::next_state before every recursive step
  during emission (morphotactics.rs:66-80). The proposer is already
  morphotactically pruned. A filter pass consulting the same index afterwards is
  looking for candidates the proposer declined to generate.

  3. This repo already measured this exact idea and wrote NO-GO.
  docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-16-candidate-prefilter-plan.md, status line:
  "Phase 0 DONE 2026-07-17 — NO-GO", census merged at 571b8a3. Its own words:

  ▎ Category (a) validity-gate rejections (the class this plan's Rust-side 
  ▎ predicate would target) are 1.5–8.1% (median ~3%) of Sena's 
  ▎ failing-candidate time, and ~2% of Indonesian's/Amharic's at comparable 
  ▎ scale — decisively under the plan's own ~10% go/no-go bar on every grammar.

  And the reason:

  ▎ Confirm cost = mrule/template unapply cascade, 88–99% of confirm time.

  The filter can only kill what it can prove impossible cheaply. The dominant
  cost is cascade dead-ends, which no cheap structural check predicts — you have
  to run the cascade to discover them. So the ceiling on this entire program,
  as designed, is roughly the 3% class that was already measured and rejected.

  It also answers the question you asked me twenty minutes ago. Same document:

  ▎ Three attempts to push precision INTO the network (flags/eliminate/compose) 
  ▎ all failed.

  Flag diacritics were tried for exactly this and didn't work. I raised them as
  unexplored prior art; they're explored and negative.

  What this doesn't kill. The reviewer identifies three things the trace
  genuinely enables that aren't in the 3% class: per-instance declination,
  runtime-overlay lexical material, and allomorph facts under a morpheme
  alphabet. Plus your circumfix design — which remains sound, but is generator
  work whose value is still unmeasured.

  What I'm doing meanwhile. I let slice 4 keep running rather than killing it,
  because its fire counts and timing are precisely the measurement that would
  confirm or refute this, and its FilterIndex, panic guard, and no-wildcard work
  are useful under any outcome. Stopping it would destroy the evidence that
  settles the argument.

  Two things need your decision, and I'd rather not guess:

● Agent "Adversarial review of filter architecture" finished · 19m 26s

● This directly contradicts what I told you, so I'm verifying it before
  relaying.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Verified, and it means I was wrong about the circumfix design. Two
  corrections, both mine:

  There is no span between halves, because there are no halves. emit.rs:377-379
  marks Role::CircumfixSuffix #[allow(dead_code)] with "Never produced by
  classify_affix." Circumfix rules route unconditionally to the
  structural-composite builder — emit.rs:1813 says "A discontinuous circumfix is
  unrepresentable by this emitter's concatenative model" — which synthesizes
  the whole word and emits one CompositeRec whose variants is the finished
  surface string. pa-batid-an is a single indivisible lexc entry. Agreement is
  enforced at grammar-compile time, so there is no mismatched candidate to 
  filter. My state-explosion justification described a span that doesn't exist.

  And "flags were tried and failed" was too broad. The three foma failures were
  matched-context flags inside ->; circumfix agreement is an inserted flag with
  left-context shape, which precision.rs:37-51 found sound because the
  conditioning material is already consumed. Flags are the more promising route
  here, not a closed door.

  The reviewer's result on your agreement key is worth reading twice: it's sound
  (over-acceptance can never lose an analysis, so soundness is free) but exact 
  only when the compatibility graph is a disjoint union of complete bipartite 
  blocks. With the full 4-tuple cross product the graph is complete bipartite —
  so the key is exact and the pass rejects zero candidates. It gains content
  only under pruning, which is exactly what destroys the biclique property.
  Minimal counterexample: (p1,s1),(p1,s2),(p2,s2),(p2,s3) — s2 forces
  key(p1)=key(p2), licensing the undeclared (p1,s3). The fix preserves zero
  grammar knowledge: emit a set of keys per half and check the intersection is
  non-empty, which is exact for any bipartite relation.

  So the real problem is entry count, not state count — N×M prefix/suffix
  allomorphs materialized at emit time. Emit-and-match doesn't touch it. Only
  declining to materialize the cross product (N+M) creates the need for an
  agreement mechanism at all, and that's emitter work with a left-context
  inserted flag, not a filter pass.

  And this is lap three: knob-teardown-plan.md records AllFlags costing 8.4x
  lexc and 4.5x compile on Sena to move precision 0.0504→0.0506 — nil — which
  made Strip permanent and handed precision work to a post-propose pre-filter,
  which was measured NO-GO the next day. Neither measurement has been retired.

      787  ```

❯ I believe there are 10 or so filters that may be possible - it is really a    
  cost measurement - do it in the FST, do it in a filter, do it in HC.  In my   
  mind, SOME MAY be better in a filter, that would be a different architecture  
  than an FST.  Divvun already does this for some things (imperfectly) and that 
  is fine for our case - HC is perfect.  Filters make HC faster.                

● That's the right frame, and it sharpens the measurement better than what I
  just wrote into the plan. Let me fix it there.

  The July NO-GO isn't "filters don't work" — it's one data point about one
  placement for one class. The general question is per-constraint: FST, filter,
  or HC. And the three have genuinely different cost shapes:

  - FST — paid at compile time (entry count, state count) plus traversal; the
  candidate is never proposed.
  - Filter — paid per candidate proposed; the HC cascade is skipped.
  - HC — paid per candidate, full cascade, always correct.

  Which means a filter wins exactly when both hold: the constraint can't go into
  the FST cheaply, and what it kills would otherwise be expensive in HC. The
  July result is really a statement about the second condition — validity-gate
  rejections fail cheaply in HC, so predicting them saves little. The reviewer's
  ownership finding is the same shape at its limit: pin-failing candidates
  continue before any parse, so they cost ~zero and filtering them saves ~zero.

  So the measurement isn't "beat 3%." It's: attribute HC cascade time by failure
  reason, then ask which reasons a cheap check could predict. A constraint
  earns a filter only if its candidates are expensive to reject in HC.

● Plan committed (597f71c) with the placement framing, and slices 5–10 are now
  gated behind the census rather than sequencing off each other.

  The census extends prefilter_census.rs (already merged at 571b8a3) rather than
  rebuilding it, and its output is a placement table: per constraint, is it
  cheap in the FST, can a cheap post-propose check predict it, and how much
  cascade time do its candidates actually consume. That third column is the one
  nobody has measured for the constraints beyond validity gates — and it's the
  only column that can justify a filter.

  Two agents still running: slice 4 (FilterIndex, structural passes — I can see
  index.rs, passes/structural.rs, and the model-check test in its working tree)
  and slice 3c (fixtures). I'm letting both finish. Slice 4's FilterIndex, panic
  guard, and no-wildcard work survive any placement outcome, and its fire
  counts feed directly into the census. 3c's fixtures are the per-constraint
  word sets the census needs to attribute against.

  On your ~10 candidate filters: the census is what sorts them, but the
  architecture review already ranks several as unlikely to pay — ownership and
  malformed-identity fire but save nothing (their candidates already skip
  parsing), slot-order and structural-transition duplicate pruning the emitter
  already does. The ones it flags as genuinely reachable only through the trace
  are narrower: per-instance declination, runtime-overlay lexical material, and
  allomorph facts under a morpheme alphabet. Those are the three I'd point the
  census at first, since they're the ones no other placement can reach.

  Worth stating plainly: your Divvun framing is what makes this tractable.
  Because HC is authoritative, every filter is optional and any of them can be
  dropped on evidence without touching correctness — so the census can be
  ruthless. The risk isn't a wrong filter, it's ten filters that each cost more
  than they save.

● Confirmed, and it's a recall bug rather than a performance one.
  pg-grammar/src/compile/affixes.rs:60-67: if an entry's morph type is
  Circumfix, the compiler pushes a warning and returns None — entry skipped. The
  module header says it outright: "circumfix cross-products are not implemented
  (warned, no rule)."

  So on the FieldWorks path, circumfix entries produce no rule at all, and words
  requiring them simply don't analyze. That's honest (it warns) but it's
  missing coverage, not slow coverage — and it's exactly what your in-flight
  cover-circumfix-cross-product-and-infix-drop change targets, so it's known
  rather than newly discovered. Worth noting it sits underneath the filter
  question: there's no partner pass to build because on one path the halves are
  fused, and on the other the entry is dropped entirely.

  Slice 3c accepted. Eight authored fixtures plus the partner-pairing
  declaration, 5 harness tests green, and -Mode conformance-test -Scope all at
  1960/1960 passed.

  The oracle discipline is the best of any slice so far: 82 signatures driven
  from pg_parse::Morpher::parse_word, transcribed verbatim, then
  programmatically re-verified against the raw dump — 8/8 fixtures, 0 
  mismatches. And all 8 grammars are well-formed XML under a strict parser,
  which 18 of the 25 pre-existing staged fixtures are not.

  - The Off/Enforce comparison is currently near-vacuous — zero passes exist, so
  both retain everything. It added non-vacuity assertions so it can't go green
  having filtered nothing, and said so rather than reporting five passing tests
  as coverage.

  slot-order overlap, because a MorphotacticIndex rich enough to model template

❯ Are the candidates to HC checks really the same?  one candidate does not only 
  take HC 300 ns - it may take ms or more.  I want the claims actually wired    
  in - removing one candidate that is poorly constrained (but has enough to     
  reject it) may do a lot rather than having hc try 10,000 rule applicaitons to

● You're right, and the arithmetic makes it stark. At ~250 ns/evaluation, with
  Sena's 51.5 candidates/word and two passes, filtering costs ~25 µs/word.
  Avoiding a single 1 ms candidate pays for 4,000 evaluations — so it breaks
  even if the filter removes one expensive candidate per ~40 words. That's a low
  bar, and my aggregate framing hid it completely. Mean cost per candidate is
  the wrong statistic when the distribution is heavy-tailed, and cascade
  dead-ends guarantee it is.

  Let me separate what survives that correction from what doesn't.

  Survives: the ownership pass specifically. confirm_batch_impl resolves pins
  and continues past failures before any parse begins — that's a control-flow
  fact, not a distribution claim. Those candidates cost ~0 in HC no matter how
  the tail is shaped, so predicting them saves ~0. Same for ExploreMode::Pruned
  candidates, which are never proposed at all.

  Does not survive: my general "filter cost versus saving" reasoning for every
  other pass. Any pass that removes candidates which actually enter the cascade
  could pay for itself many times over on a single word, and no synthetic model
  check can tell us whether it does.

  Which means the honest measurement is the one you're asking for — wired in,
  against real grammars, correlating each would-be death against the HC time
  that candidate actually consumed. That's precisely what shadow mode does:
  compute the deaths, send everything to HC anyway, then attribute. Slice 9
  isn't blocked by the census; it's the instrument that produces it.

  Dispatching it now, in its own worktree so it doesn't collide with the fixture
  wiring still running.

● Slice 9 dispatched as the census instrument rather than as a plan step. Two
  things in its brief follow directly from your point:

  It must report the distribution, not a mean. Total, median, p90, max, and
  specifically the count of would-be-killed candidates whose HC cost was 
  effectively zero. That last number is what separates a pass that removes
  expensive candidates from one mirroring work HC already skips — and it's the
  only way the ownership null result and a genuine win look different in the
  data.

  It must attribute honestly or not at all. confirm_batch_impl groups candidates
  by root set before parsing, so per-candidate attribution may not be cleanly
  available. I told it to report the finest attribution it can actually achieve
  and say what that does and doesn't capture — explicitly not to restructure
  confirm_batch_impl in this slice, and never to fabricate a per-candidate
  figure from a group total. A measurement that overstates its own precision is
  worse than a coarse one that's honest about it.

  I also asked for a deterministic proxy alongside the wall clock — HC steps or
  rule applications — since the repo's own evidence rules make timing
  inadmissible except where time is the subject. Here it is the subject, but a
  reproducible counter is what anyone can re-check later.

● Agent "Wire passes to fixtures and measure" finished · 22m 30s

● A structural finding, and the design error is mine.

  The fixture harness feeds the filter analyses that HC already accepted.
  adapt() is fed morpher.parse_word(w).structured — the confirmed output. Since
  a sound filter may never reject what HC accepts, no sound pass can ever fire 
  in that harness, on any grammar. It's a logical impossibility, not a tuning
  problem, and I specified it in slice 3c's brief.

  So both measured fire counts are 0 against estimates of 2 and 3, and the gaps
  aren't the fixtures' fault. The rows authored to provoke rejections are
  expect_fail rows yielding zero analyses, so the impossible candidates never
  get proposed at all. There's no proposer in the loop.

  Three consequences, all stated plainly by the agent rather than papered over:

  - structural.transition.v1 cannot fire through the legacy adapter on any 
  grammar. It reads from.slot.known() before touching FilterIndex; the adapter
  defers slot, so every adjacent pair returns Undecidable and the pass defers.
  The 67% defer rate confirms it — and the 19 keeps are exactly the
  single-morpheme witnesses where windows(2) is empty.
  - Ownership's 0% defer rate is the opposite of decisiveness. All 57 witnesses
  are pin-resolvable, so it returns Keep at its first branch and never reaches
  any decision logic. Zero defers because zero decisions.
  - The Off/Enforce agreement is vacuous. It passes over all 57 analyses, but
  with 0 rejections it only shows the passes aren't lossy. The property worth
  having — a pass rejects and the comparison still holds — has not been
  demonstrated.

  And the honest kicker: min_fire_count: 0 makes the fire-count gate assert 0 >=
  0, which can never fail. The agent flagged that rather than banking the
  green.

  The population a filter can act on is candidates HC will reject — and only the
  FST proposer emits those. That's precisely what slice 9 wires, and why it's
  the only instrument that can close this. It's already running.

  Fixing the vacuous gate now rather than leaving it, since it's the exact
  failure mode I've been holding every other slice to.

● Agent(Close the vacuous fire-count gate)  ⎿  Backgrounded agent (↓ to manage · ctrl+o to expand)

● Two agents running: the gate fix in candidate-filter, shadow integration in
  candidate-filter-shadow.

  Where the chain actually stands, stated without varnish:

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────┐
  │              Component              │               State               │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Contract, model, pipeline, ledger   │ Built, warning-free, ~150 tests   │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Post-hoc verification               │ Built, 106 forgery cases          │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ FilterIndex + 2 structural passes   │ Built, model-checked, ~250        │
  │                                     │ ns/eval                           │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 8 per-pass fixtures,                │ Built, 1960/1960 under -Scope all │
  │ oracle-verified                     │                                   │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Filter wired between FST and HC     │ In progress — slice 9             │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Evidence any pass earns its keep    │ None yet                          │
  └─────────────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────┘

  That last row is the honest summary. Everything built so far is machinery
  whose value is still unmeasured, and the two attempts to measure it have both
  hit the same wall from different sides: the fixture harness can't produce
  candidates HC would reject, and the legacy adapter defers the facts the
  transition pass needs. Slice 9 fixes the first — the FST proposer emits
  exactly the population a filter can act on. Only a new generator fixes the
  second.

  Two structural facts now well-established that will shape whatever comes next:

  Passes split by what they need. Ownership-class passes need no trace facts but
  target candidates HC already skips before parsing. Transition/slot/span-class
  passes could target expensive candidates but need Known facts the current
  producer cannot supply. Neither group can pay for itself today, for opposite
  reasons — and that's a cleaner statement of the problem than "the filter is or
  isn't worth it."

  Your heavy-tail point is what keeps it open. If the FST proposer emits even a
  few candidates that cost HC milliseconds, and a pass can prove them impossible
  from facts the proposer already has, the economics work at 250 ns/eval. Slice
  9's distribution — median, p90, max, and the zero-cost count — is the number
  that decides it. Not a percentage.

● The gate fix landed (8de3f5f), plus one follow-up of mine (b21e1f6), 6/6
  green.

  The agent's design here is better than what I asked for. I wanted a status
  that describes reality; it built one that self-invalidates. producer-blocked
  isn't an exemption — a_producer_blocked_fixture_still_reaches_nothing
  re-measures both passes on every build and fails the moment either reaches a 
  single rejection, forcing promotion to wired with a real floor. A parked
  fixture carries a live assertion rather than a waiver.

  It also falsified the anti-rot gate in both directions with real probes, not
  just the one I specified: a built pass with an awaiting-pass fixture fires,
  and an unbuilt pass mis-parked as producer-blocked fires with its own distinct
  message. Then reverted both probes.

  The closed reason set is exactly two members, and they name the two real
  blocks precisely:
  - producer-emits-only-hc-confirmed-analyses
  - adapter-defers-a-fact-the-pass-reads-first

  structural-transition carries both, because it's blocked twice over. Neither
  estimate was lowered — ownership keeps its floor of 2, transition its 3, to be
  enforced once a producer exists.

✻ Cooked for 2m 7s

❯ One thing we need to keep in mind is that this may enable us making a         
  smaller, simpler FST.  The gain may not only be reaslized as "makes HC        
  faster" but "enables us to shrink the FST without making inference (much)     
  slower"                                                                       

● That reframes the economics, and it specifically undoes part of my "vacuous"
  verdict — I should say which part.

  I argued StructuralTransitionPass duplicates work the emitter already does,
  because ExploreMode::Pruned consults MorphotacticIndex before every recursive
  step. That's only true if you keep the pruning. If the pass lets you turn
  pruning off, it isn't duplication — it's substitution, and the gain shows up
  as a smaller, faster-to-build FST rather than as HC time saved.

  And the lever for testing that already exists. ExploreMode::Flat — "skip the
  automaton entirely" — is kept in the source explicitly for A/B measurement,
  reachable via HC_PREEXPAND_FLAT=1. So the experiment is: build Flat, measure
  the FST size and compile-time drop plus the extra candidates, then check
  whether the filter absorbs those candidates for less than pruning cost.

  It also gives your circumfix design its real justification. The architecture
  review concluded emit-and-match doesn't help because "the cost is real but is
  entry count, not state count… only stopping the materialization (N+M instead 
  of N×M) creates the need for an agreement mechanism." It treated that as a
  reason not to build the filter. Under your framing it's the opposite: the
  filter is what makes stopping the materialization possible. N×M composite
  entries collapse to N+M, and the agreement check moves out of the grammar
  compile into a few dozen lines of stack matching.

  Recording this as a second measurement axis, because it's a design-level fact
  that needs to outlive this conversation.

● Committed (4bc7f36). The census now has two axes rather than one.

  The distinction that matters: on axis 1 a pass must remove candidates that are
  expensive in HC. On axis 2 it only has to be cheaper than the proposer-side
  machinery it replaces — and that's a much easier bar, because it's competing
  against compile time and entry count rather than against a cascade that may
  never run.

  ExploreMode::Flat is the cleanest test available. It's already in the source,
  already kept for A/B, and it isolates exactly one precision mechanism. Build
  both ways, compare artifact size, compile time, and candidate count, then ask
  whether StructuralTransitionPass absorbs the extra candidates for less than
  pruning costs. That's a decisive experiment on a pass I'd written off as
  duplicative — and it needs no new machinery.

  Two things worth flagging about this axis before anyone gets optimistic:

  It only works for passes that can actually fire. StructuralTransitionPass
  currently defers unconditionally because it reads from.slot.known() and the
  adapter defers slot. Under Flat, does the proposer emit slot facts? If not,
  the pass can't absorb what Flat lets through, and the substitution fails on
  availability rather than on economics. That's the first thing the experiment
  should check.

  The July evidence cuts both ways here. AllFlags cost 8.4x lexc and 4.5x
  compile on Sena for a precision change of 0.0504 → 0.0506 — nil. That was
  measured going toward precision-in-the-FST, and it's the strongest existing
  evidence that FST-side precision is expensive. Which is encouraging for axis
  2: if precision in the network costs that much, removing it should give back
  real money. The open question is whether Flat's extra candidates are cheap for
  the filter and cheap for HC, or whether they're the pathological ones.

  Slice 9 is still the gate for axis 1 and still running. Axis 2 is a separate
  experiment I'd run after it lands, since it needs the filter wired to measure
  the absorption cost at all.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Both pieces already exist, which changes what needs building.

  The A/B harness is mostly there. backend_space.rs has StageMeasurement,
  Quantiles, PilotSummary, summarize_pilot(measurements, seed), and
  deterministic_sample_indices(population, sample, seed) — deterministic
  sampling with quantile summarisation, which is exactly the shape the
  heavy-tail measurement needs and exactly what you don't want to hand-roll. It
  also has Characterization / SpaceCounts / FeasibleCount for enumerating an
  alternatives space with reconciliation. The iteration harness is a consumer of
  that, not a new thing.

  And the pack field has a home already. PackManifest.fst_health is a
  pg_foma::health::HealthReport, reused verbatim. That vocabulary has Metric,
  MetricValue, ValueProvenance, FindingCode, Remedy, and OverrideRecord — so a
  placement decision is a health finding with provenance, not a new manifest
  concept. The compile/report/health cycle you described is the thing that
  already writes it.

  On reversing the no-pack-config decision — the reason is sound, and it's worth
  naming precisely. What you're proposing isn't configuration in the sense we
  rejected. It's a record of a build decision: whether this FST compiled in the
  check. That is genuinely not derivable from the grammar at load time, because
  it's a property of how the artifact was built, not of what the grammar says.
  Deriving-from-grammar can't recover it even in principle.

  The safety argument is what makes it the right call, and it's unusually
  strong. Every possible value of a filter-skip list yields correct results:

  ┌──────────────┬──────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Field says  │ FST has the  │                 Outcome                  │
  │              │    check     │                                          │
  ├──────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ skip         │ yes          │ correct, redundant work avoided          │
  ├──────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ skip         │ no           │ filter doesn't run, HC rejects instead — │
  │              │              │  slower, still correct                   │
  ├──────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ nothing      │ either       │ all filters run, possibly redundant —    │
  │              │              │ correct                                  │
  ├──────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ unknown      │ either       │ skip is skip — correct                   │
  │ filter id    │              │                                          │
  └──────────────┴──────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┘

  A corrupt, stale, or even adversarial value costs speed and nothing else.
  That's the exact opposite risk direction from the shipped-index idea we
  rejected, where a wrong value could make a filter reject something valid. Skip
  can only make a filter not run, and HC is authoritative. So this field is
  safe by construction in a way almost nothing else in a pack is — which is
  precisely why it's the one thing worth putting there.

  One caveat for when it's built: the skip list must be advisory, never a
  requirement. If a pack names a filter the runtime lacks, that's a no-op, not a
  load failure — it must never reach required_runtime_features, for the same
  reason as before.

● Verified — and this is the structural finding that most changes the economics.
  if doomed_members == chunk.members.len(): a chunk's HC steps count as
  removable only when every member would die. confirm_batch_impl fuses
  candidates into chunks by root set and parses the chunk, so killing some
  members doesn't avoid the parse.
