● Clean fast-forward — the chain is complete at efc3813. FST proposer →
  candidate filter → HC-rust confirmation, wired through a single
  filter_then_confirm helper with cost attribution on the confirmation side.
  Full suite running in the background.

● The chain is closed. What it produced, and what it means:

  The instrument.
  analyzer.analyze_word_with_diagnostics(word).diagnostics.filter now carries a
  ShadowCostAttribution that partitions would-be-killed candidates into
  not_presented / never_grouped / sole_member / shared_member, and reports the
  distribution over exactly-attributable ones — median, p90, max, and
  exact_zero_cost. Raw per-chunk records are public via
  confirm_batch_attributed.

  It refuses to over-claim. A candidate sharing a chunk has no derivable share,
  and the agent declined to invent one — those land in would_die_shared_member
  and their chunk's cost in surviving_chunk_steps. It also noted that
  ParseOutcome::steps prices the restricted reparse and doesn't separate the
  mrule/template cascade from lexical lookup, so it cannot on its own confirm
  the 88–99% cascade figure. Both are the right calls.

  The null result is now mechanized rather than argued. Zero rejections through
  the legacy adapter, with the reason pinned by test: StructuralTransitionPass
  needs slot and stratum Known on both units of a pair and the adapter defers
  both, so it is structurally unable to reject on any grammar; OwnershipPass's
  only fact-free paths sit behind a pins_resolve failure the tag-decoding
  proposer never produces. 6 witnesses, 12 evaluations, 5 defers, 0 rejections —
  the passes really ran and really decided nothing.

  And the finding that most changes the economics. Enforcement only deletes work
  when an entire chunk dies. Killing some members of a root-set group still
  runs the parse. So the target isn't expensive candidates, it's expensive
  groups whose every member is provably impossible — a much narrower population
  than anything we'd been reasoning about.

  One caveat that governs all of it: this worktree has no private corpora, so
  every number so far is synthetic on a population of one. The single measurable
  data point — a would-die candidate costing HC zero steps, 18.6 µs of call
  setup and nothing else — reproduces the "fails cheaply" shape of the July
  NO-GO, but on a toy grammar it's a demonstration that the instrument works,
  not evidence about real grammars.

  Next is running it under -Mode corpus-test against the five grammars, which
  needs PANGLOSS_CORPUS_ROOT. I'll report the suite result when it finishes.

✻ Worked for 3h 40m 5s

❯ Yes, candidates are grouped - I am unsure whether it is better to filter pre  
  or post grouping.  I would assume: deduplicate first and then filter and then 
  group.                                                                        

● Good question, and the current code already answers half of it — let me check
  the other half rather than assume.

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

● Your proposed order is already what's built. composite.rs:7 — "Pipeline:
  propose(word) UNION peel_candidates(word, propose), deduped by …" — and line
  50 calls them "the deduped candidates this word was proposed for, in the order
  confirmation will see them." So today it's propose → dedup → filter → group,
  exactly as you'd have it.

  But the chunk finding suggests a better order, and it falls out of two facts
  that fit together neatly.

  Grouping is nearly free, because pin resolution already produces the key.
  confirm_batch_impl resolves pins first — that's the continue-before-parse step
  — and the root set it computes is the grouping key. So the grouping is a
  by-product of work that already happens unconditionally.

  And a group with any survivor runs regardless. Verified: doomed_members == 
  chunk.members.len() is the only condition under which steps become removable.
  So in Enforce mode, once one member of a group survives the filter, filtering 
  the rest of that group is wasted work — the chunk parses either way.

  Which gives:

  propose → dedup → resolve pins → group → filter each group, stop at first
  survivor → confirm

  Cost goes from N candidates × P passes to roughly
  (members-until-first-survivor) summed over groups. For a group that survives,
  often one candidate. For a doomed group, all of them — but that's the case
  where you actually collect the saving.

  It also explains OwnershipPass cleanly: pin resolution rejects those
  candidates for free and is required for grouping anyway. You cannot beat free,
  which is why that pass measures zero rather than small.

  Three caveats before anyone builds it:

  - Early exit is Enforce-only. Shadow mode needs every candidate evaluated to
  attribute cost, and slice 10's Off-vs-Enforce comparison needs
  order-independent evidence. Two modes, two behaviours — worth keeping explicit
  rather than deriving from the mode, given how the depth knob went.
  - It makes the ledger order-dependent. Which candidates get evaluated now
  depends on group iteration order, and slice 9 already had to sort chunk
  membership because the loop iterates an FxHashMap. Determinism is pinned by
  test, so this would have to preserve it.
  - The saving is unmeasured and measurable now. Whether failures cluster in
  doomed groups or scatter across surviving ones is exactly what
  would_die_shared_member versus removable_chunks reports. That's a corpus
  question, and the instrument for it exists.

  I'd hold the reordering until the corpus census runs. If failures scatter
  across surviving groups, early exit saves most of the filter cost but the
  filter saves nothing anyway; if they cluster, both numbers matter. Same
  measurement decides both.

● Found the scaffolding — prefilter_census.rs (the July harness), plus
  backend_candidate_census.rs and deadend_census.rs to model on.

  But there's a problem with the census as I framed it: the structural passes
  provably kill nothing through the legacy adapter, so a shadow run would
  measure an empty set. The fix makes the measurement far more valuable —
  measure the ceiling instead of the current passes.

  In shadow mode every candidate still goes to HC, so we already learn which
  ones got zero confirmations. Those are exactly the candidates a perfect filter
  could have removed. Feeding that set through the chunk attribution answers
  the question that bounds the entire program: if an ideal filter existed, how 
  much HC work would actually disappear? Given that a chunk only becomes
  removable when its whole membership dies, that ceiling may be far below the
  ~95% candidate failure rate.

● Agent(Run the ceiling census on corpora)
  ⎿  Backgrounded agent (↓ to manage · ctrl+o to expand)

● Census running. Indonesian first, 20 words, then expand.

  The measurement is the ceiling, not the current passes — which makes it
  independent of anything we've built and far more decisive. The headline is one
  ratio:

  removable_steps / (removable_steps + surviving_chunk_steps)

  That's the fraction of confirmation work a perfect filter could delete. If
  it's small, no filter design helps and the program stops on evidence rather
  than on argument.

  I also asked for the gap between two numbers that sound alike and aren't:
  candidates with empty buckets (what an ideal filter could kill) versus chunks 
  that actually became removable (what that would delete). Sena's 5% candidate
  precision means ~95% of candidates fail — but if those failures scatter across
  chunks that each retain one survivor, the removable fraction is near zero.
  That gap is the whole question, and nobody has measured it.

  Plus the member-count distribution of removable chunks, because a chunk
  removable at size 1 is a much easier target than one where all eight members
  happened to fail — different filters, different difficulty.

  On safety: this repo has three recorded exhaustion events (97 GB, 90 GB, 118
  GB), all from probes invoked outside the managed path. So it runs through
  pg.ps1 -Mode run for the kernel-enforced job object, single-threaded, with a
  per-word timeout treated as a typed outcome rather than an empty result.
  Amharic and Aweti come last if at all, given their documented timeout hazards.

  ├──────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ Mbugwe, Aweti        │ no data — see below               │ —           │
  └──────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────┴─────────────┘

  Sena's 93.6% is real and large, and bit-identical across two independent runs
  while wall clock moved 2044→2610 ms — steps are the reproducible unit, as the
  repo's evidence rules assume.

  Indonesian's 15% is one word. 54 of its 68 removable steps come from
  mengamat-amati; without it, 4.8%. The other 14 removable chunks are 1 step
  each.

  Amharic's two numbers disagree 10x — 32% by steps, 3.7% by time — because its
  surviving chunks are where the time actually goes (2618 ms surviving vs 101 ms
  removable). Its steps figure badly overstates the ceiling. That divergence is
  a warning about trusting steps alone.

  The gap that matters

  Killable candidates are not deletable work:

  ┌────────────┬─────────────────────┬─────────────────────┬──────────────┐
  │            │ Candidates killable │ Chunks fully doomed │ Work deleted │
  ├────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Indonesian │ 30.2%               │ 12.7%               │ 15.1%        │
  ├────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Sena       │ 97.8%               │ 82.9%               │ 93.6%        │
  ├────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Amharic    │ 71.8%               │ 29.0%               │ 3.7% (wall)  │
  └────────────┴─────────────────────┴─────────────────────┴──────────────┘

  Counting killable candidates overstates achievable saving by 2.3x on 
  Indonesian and 19x on Amharic. Only Sena translates.

  The engineering problem this creates

  Removable-chunk member counts are opposite between grammars:

  - Indonesian: 80% singletons, median 1, max 3 — easy to kill, worth 1 step
  each.
  - Sena: median 8, p90 34, max 335 — where all the money is, and you must kill
  every member of a chunk to collect any of it.

  So a pass with 90% coverage on a 20-member Sena chunk deletes nothing. The
  grammar where filtering pays is the one where partial coverage is worthless.
  That's a much harder target than "reject more candidates," and it's the
  constraint any future pass has to be designed against.

  Two things not to over-read

  Mbugwe and Aweti produced zero candidates — the harness uses emit::emit, and
  those grammars need the templated/composite path. Their ceiling is unmeasured,
  not zero.

  Your 20-word suggestion didn't work as expected, through no fault of the plan:
  the Indonesian list is alphabetical, so the first 20 lines are all bare
  uninflected roots that every candidate confirms — zero denominator, no
  ceiling. Derived forms start around line 33. The agent expanded to 121 and
  reported the degenerate first run verbatim rather than quietly re-slicing,
  which is the right call.

  One outstanding verification

  The census committed production changes (ConfirmChunkCost.timed_out,
  shadow::attribute made public, chunk_is_removable extracted) that have not 
  been run against the 960-test suite. pg.ps1 refuses with exit 17: a dotnet
  process (pid 35768) is holding 44.12 GB, commit charge is at 98% of limit, and
  only 2.9 GB is available against the 8 GB reserve. That's not my process and
  I won't kill it or override the gate — it exists because this machine was
  taken to zero memory twice. The code has compiled cleanly six times through
  the managed release path; the suite run is pending machine headroom.

  Minor doc/code mismatch spotted: CLAUDE.md says gc is exempt from the memory
  gate "because it is the recovery action," but -Mode gc refused with exit 17
  too.

● Right — and the percentages actively mislead here. Let me convert to absolutes
  from the aggregates I have, then get the proper distribution.

  Sena: 101,847 steps over 100 words = ~1,018 steps/word. Removing doomed chunks
  leaves 6,477 → ~65 steps/word. That's a 15.7x reduction. Against your ~100
  ms/word estimate that lands near 6.4 ms/word — very close to the 7 ms you
  guessed.

  Indonesian: 451 steps over 121 words = 3.7 steps/word. A 15% cut removes ~0.6
  steps/word. There is nothing to win; it's already fast, exactly as you say.

  Amharic: 288 steps over 60 words = 4.8 steps/word, and the removable ones are
  the cheap ones (101 ms removable vs 2,618 ms surviving). Filtering would shave
  the fast cases and leave the slow ones.

  And your 5 ms filter estimate is probably 10–50x too high. Measured pass
  evaluation is ~250 ns. Even at Sena's 51.5 candidates/word with several passes
  and longer real traces, that's tens to hundreds of microseconds per word —
  call it 0.1–0.5 ms. Against ~100 ms/word, the filter is close to free, which
  makes the p50/p99 trade you describe strictly better than you framed it.

  That said, two things I genuinely don't have and shouldn't fake: per-word 
  distributions (the census reported per-chunk steps) and a meaningful p99 — 100
  words gives you one word at p99, which is noise.

      1610 +function Get-StaleWorktreeCandidates {                       
      1611 +    <#                                                       
      1612 +      .DESCRIPTION                                           
      1613 +      Worktrees whose disk is reclaimable: every change commi
           +tted, and no commit for -IdleDays.                           
      1614 +                                                             
      1615 +      Removing a worktree does not delete its branch, so comm
           +itted work survives and is recoverable                       
      1616 +      with a plain checkout. The only thing a removal can des
           +troy is uncommitted or untracked                             
      1617 +      content -- which is exactly why a worktree with ANY dir
           +ty or untracked path is never listed                         
      1618 +      here, however old it is. A judgement call on one of tho
           +se belongs to a human.                                       
      1619 +                                                             
      1620 +      Deliberately does not measure directory sizes: this run
           +s on a refusal path, and walking every                       
      1621 +      file of dozens of checkouts would add minutes to a mess
           +age whose whole purpose is to be read                        
      1622 +      quickly. Size varies mostly with leftover build output,
           + so the oldest are usually the largest.                      
      1623 +    #>                                                       
      1624 +    param(                                                   
      1625 +        [Parameter(Mandatory)][string]$RepoRoot,             
      1626 +        [int]$IdleDays = 3                                   
      1627 +    )                                                        
      1628 +    $found = @()                                             
      1629 +    $listing = & git -C $RepoRoot worktree list --porcelain 2
           +>$null                                                       
      1630 +    if ($LASTEXITCODE -ne 0) { return $found }               
      1631 +    $cutoff = (Get-Date).AddDays(-$IdleDays)                 
      1632 +    foreach ($line in $listing) {                            
      1633 +        if ($line -notmatch '^worktree (.+)$') { continue }  
      1634 +        $path = $Matches[1]                                  
      1635 +        if ((Resolve-Path -LiteralPath $path -ErrorAction Sil
           +entlyContinue).ProviderPath -eq (Resolve-Path -LiteralPath $R
           +epoRoot).ProviderPath) { continue }                          
      1636 +        $dirty = & git -C $path status --porcelain --untracke
           +d-files=all 2>$null                                          
      1637 +        if ($LASTEXITCODE -ne 0 -or $dirty) { continue }     
      1638 +        $stamp = & git -C $path log -1 --format=%cI 2>$null  
      1639 +        if ($LASTEXITCODE -ne 0 -or [string]::IsNullOrWhiteSp
           +ace($stamp)) { continue }                                    
      1640 +        $when = $null                                        
      1641 +        if (-not [datetime]::TryParse($stamp, [ref]$when)) { 
           +continue }                                                   
      1642 +        if ($when -ge $cutoff) { continue }                  
      1643 +        $found += [PSCustomObject]@{                         
      1644 +            Path     = $path                                 
      1645 +            Name     = Split-Path $path -Leaf                
      1646 +            Branch   = (& git -C $path rev-parse --abbrev-ref
           + HEAD 2>$null)                                               
      1647 +            IdleDays = [int]((Get-Date) - $when).TotalDays   
      1648 +        }                                                    
      1649 +    }                                                        
      1650 +    return @($found | Sort-Object IdleDays -Descending)      
      1651 +}

❯ when we remove a worktree, we should also cleanup the                         
  C:/cargo-targets/<worktree-name>.  How can this be automatic?                 

● The gc fix works — it just ran at 0.6 GB available where it previously
  refused. And it surfaced something bigger than the worktrees.

  The target directories dwarf the checkouts. crp-ci-clippy is 1.63 GB of
  worktree but 8.05 + 2.51 GB of target dirs. crp-round2-root-entry 6.52 GB,
  crp-ci-assess 6.37 GB, profiler-health 5.7 GB. And gc marks every one of them
  "live" solely because the worktree still exists — so removing the worktree is
  what unlocks reclaiming them. Your 10 GB estimate is low by an order of
  magnitude; this is likely 60–100 GB.

  To your question: git has no worktree-removal hook, so it can't be automatic
  inside git. The right place is the tool that created the pairing.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● gc already has the whole seam — Get-ManagedTargetDirs,
  Get-TargetClassification, Invoke-TargetGc, plus the ownership markers. So the
  automatic answer is a remove-worktree mode that mirrors new-worktree: the tool
  that creates the worktree/target-dir pairing is the one that should destroy
  it, calling the same reclamation gc uses rather than duplicating the logic. gc
  -Apply then stays the safety net for worktrees removed by hand — it already
  classifies those targets as disposable once the worktree is gone.

  Checking my edits are isolated before committing, since this checkout has 34
  of your dirty files.

  Three jobs dispatched, each with the constraint that made the manual pass
  work:

  remove-worktree mode — deletes the directory and prunes, because git worktree 
  remove is simply unusable here (submodules), and then reclaims only that 
  slug's disposable target dirs via gc's existing classification rather than a
  copy of it. Refuses outright on any uncommitted or untracked file, with no
  -Force — committed work survives via the branch, uncommitted work doesn't, and
  that call is yours.

  A gc switch so one command does everything, dry-run by default like the rest
  of gc, routing through the same removal path.

  A find hook on the block-bare-cargo.py pattern. Worth being explicit about why
  ignore files can't solve this: find honours none of them, and
  C:\cargo-targets / G:\cargo-build-cache sit outside the repo anyway — so the
  refusal message says that, to stop the next person trying.

  exist for an untyped [ref], and my first version of the disk message advised

  Two structural constraints that shape everything:

  1. Only whole doomed chunks free work. Sena's removable chunks have median 8
  members, max 335 — a pass with 90% coverage on a 20-member chunk deletes
  nothing. Where the money is, partial coverage is worthless.
  2. Killable candidates ≠ deletable work. Amharic: 71.8% of candidates killable
  buys 3.7% of time. Counting candidates overstates the win by up to 19x.

  No pass has fired, ever, and two structurally cannot through the legacy
  adapter — StructuralTransitionPass reads slot.known() first and the adapter
  defers it; OwnershipPass's fact-free paths need a pin failure the proposer
  never emits. Both pinned by test rather than argued.

  What was blocked is now unblocked. The per-word p50/p90/p99 distribution code
  is committed but never executed — the .NET process tripped exit 17. Memory now
  reads 44.5 GB available. That's your tail-latency question, so I'm running
  it.

● Agent(Investigate chunk reparse cost shape) Sonnet 5
  ⎿  Backgrounded agent (↓ to manage · ctrl+o to expand)

● Indonesian is measured; it's a null result at the tail, and for a structural 
  reason. The ideal filter moves p99 confirm time by 0.0% (2.744 ms unchanged),
  because removable chunks are 80% singletons averaging 1 reparse step while
  surviving chunks reach 104. Chunk fusion by root set means killing a candidate
  frees nothing unless every chunk-mate also dies, and in the expensive tail
  they don't. The good news is on the cost side: the filter measures 0.001 
  ms/word at p50, 0.013 ms worst case — not the ~5 ms you'd budgeted, and three
  orders of magnitude below my own estimate. So the trade is never "p50 up a few
  ms"; the filter is free enough to be invisible.

  That reframes the program's central question, so I've put it in flight rather
  than waiting: is the fused chunk reparse cost per-candidate or per-root-set?
  If it's per-candidate, then telling HC "only K of these N are live" narrows
  the tail chunks directly, and the whole-chunk-only accounting our census
  enforces is understating the ceiling. If it's per-root-set, narrowing buys
  nothing and whole-chunk elimination is the only lever — which would make
  Indonesian's null result the expected shape for every grammar with small
  chunks. A Sonnet agent is reading confirm.rs for that now, under instructions
  to cite file:line and to report bad news plainly rather than find good news.

  Your Sena estimate was close in shape: p50 goes 6.3 → 0.9 ms and p99 355 →
  24.6 ms. But the filter costs 0.06 ms/word mean, 1.8 ms worst case ever 
  observed — not the 5 ms you budgeted, so there is no "p50 goes up a few ms"
  trade to accept anywhere. Arithmetic verified on all three runs (Sena
  1264103+89294=1353397; Amharic 1462+1455=2917; means and eval counts divide
  correctly), which matters because 8b8d8bd's reporting code had never executed
  before today.

  The one number that must not be over-read: zero candidates were rejected in 
  all three runs. These are ceilings from an oracle, not achieved results. The
  legacy adapter establishes no role, slot, or stratum fact, so every real pass
  can only defer. The ceiling is now well measured; the achieved saving is still
  0.00%.

  Three findings that change the plan more than the headline does:

  steps is not portable across grammars, and I had been treating it as if it 
  were. Amharic runs ~13.6 ms per step (111 steps = 1508 ms); Sena runs ~0.024
  ms per step (14994 steps = 355 ms) — a 570× spread. For Amharic, steps
  overstate the win badly: −64.9% on steps but only −30.8% on time. Any go/no-go
  keyed on step share would have mis-scored Amharic. Decisions go on measured
  time; steps only compare within one grammar. This is the same trap as your
  "don't just look at percentages" correction, one level down.

  On Sena, the proposer becomes the bottleneck the moment the filter works.
  Filtered confirm p99 is 24.557 ms; propose p99 is 24.875 ms — propose is now
  the larger half, and its mean (15.4 ms) is 7× the filtered confirm mean (2.2
  ms). Sena's FST emits ~178 candidates/word at 98.1% empty buckets. So
  confirm-side work on Sena is nearly exhausted, and this is direct evidence for
  your axis-2 thesis: the filter's payoff is that it licenses a sloppier, 
  smaller FST, rather than only making HC faster. It also points away from
  pushing precision into the network, which failed three times per the
  2026-07-17 NO-GO.

  Amharic's residual tail is not candidate-shaped. After ideal filtering it
  still sits at 1043.7 ms at p99 with only 39 steps. That cost is inside
  surviving steps — allomorph selection and phonology — and a filter that
  removes candidates cannot touch it. Amharic's tail is a separate program, and
  I'd stop counting it as something this work will fix.

  My verdict on the gate (task 17 was blocking slices 5–11): GO, but reordered.
  The program is justified by Sena alone and costs nothing on Indonesian. What I
  would not do is proceed straight into slices 5–7 building more passes,
  because no pass has ever fired once — the binding constraint is task 19
  (re-point at the real proposer), not pass count. Building three more passes
  that all defer would add code and zero evidence.

  Still in flight: the chunk-cost investigation (whether narrowing a chunk,
  rather than eliminating it whole, is a real lever — which decides whether
  Indonesian's null result is permanent) and the resumed find-scope hook.

● This changes the status of today's headline, so let me be precise about it.

  The agent found — with citations — that the "after" column is a model, not a 
  measurement. filter_ceiling_census.rs:272 calls confirm once, on the full
  unpruned candidate array, derives the doomed set from that run's own empty
  buckets, then computes "after" by zeroing wholly-doomed chunks and copying
  every surviving chunk's cost through unchanged. shadow.rs:319-324 states that
  as an explicit assumption. No pruned re-run happens anywhere in the script.
  This is exactly what task 17's description warned against claiming, and I
  presented it as measured — that was my error, not the census's.

  Turning the model into a measurement, since the seam already exists:

● The find hook is real and I verified it independently — chained (cd /tmp && 
  find /), subshell, and piped forms all deny; findstr correctly doesn't match;
  scoped and deep-absolute paths pass. Committed as 379093d6 with 17 tests. The
  agent disclosed its own gap honestly rather than hiding it: find -H / (a flag
  before the path) is not blocked, because distinguishing find's option flags
  from path arguments needs real argument parsing. Under-blocking was the right
  call there.

  One thing that limits it, which nobody has addressed yet: the hook lives in
  tooling-disk-recovery's .claude/settings.json. The incident it prevents — a
  root scan burning 2110 CPU-seconds outside every pg.ps1 control — happens in
  any worktree, and roughly two dozen run here. It protects one worktree until
  that branch reaches main.

  Correcting today's main result, since I stated it too strongly:

  The before/after numbers I gave you are a model on the "after" side, not a 
  measurement. The census runs confirm once on the full candidate array, then
  computes "after" by deleting wholly-doomed chunks and copying every surviving
  chunk's cost through unchanged — shadow.rs:319-324 documents that as an
  assumption. It never re-runs confirm on a pruned candidate set. So the
  Indonesian 0.0% cannot distinguish "no saving exists" from "the census never
  looked."

  The direction matters and is mostly reassuring: since surviving chunks are
  copied through unchanged, the model is conservative with respect to any
  within-chunk saving, so Sena's −93.1% is a floor on that axis, not an inflated
  figure. But there's one way it could be optimistic that I want measured:
  pruning changes each chunk's rule union, and cross-root-set fusion only merges
  chunks whose rule_filter predicates are byte-identical — so pruning can break
  a fusion and produce more parse calls than before. That would be invisible in
  the current accounting.

  A Sonnet agent is now adding a genuine pruned re-run reporting three columns
  (before / after-modelled / after-measured) plus chunk and parse-call counts,
  interleaved per word so the second run can't win on warm caches. The engine's
  design bounds how much this can recover — root-set grouping is exact equality
  and rule-union slack is capped at 3 — so I expect whole-chunk elimination to
  remain the dominant lever. But "I expect" is what got me here, so it's being
  measured.

● Agent "Scope the Known-fact generator" finished · 7m 14s

● This is the most consequential result of the day, and it kills one of your
  design ideas outright.

  Your circumfix example is not implementable as modeled. You asked me to
  validate it — "the FST should say 'this is the left hand side of circumfix A
  with phonology 2' and 'this is the right hand side with phonology 3'." The
  verified chain: classify_affix (emit.rs:401-464) evaluates one allomorph's
  whole RHS as a unit and yields a single verdict; a circumfixing rule is one
  MRuleId owning one MorphemeId; struct_extend (emit.rs:2078-2079) pushes
  exactly one (morpheme, tag) pair per rule application; and CompositeRec stores
  one already-phonology-resolved surface for the whole chain. The two halves
  never exist separately at any stage — grammar model, emission, or decode. A
  PartnerOpen/PartnerClose pair needs two trace units sharing a PartnerClassId,
  and a circumfix produces one. Making it produce two is a change to
  MorphRuleDef in pg_grammar::model, not emitter plumbing — outside this
  project's stated scope of not building the generator.

  That also takes down one of the plan's two concrete axis-2 instances:
  circumfix N×M → N+M needs halves to be separately representable, so it's
  blocked behind the same data-model change. ExploreMode::Flat is the only
  axis-2 instance still standing.

  And the tape is far poorer than I assumed. apply_up returns opaque strings;
  decode_path scans them for <R:nnnn>/<M:nnnn> only. The entire payload reaching
  a candidate is a MorphemeId sequence plus which position is the root.
  TraceRole, TraceSlotId, TraceStratumId, PartnerClassId, SurfaceSpan,
  LocalEvent, AllomorphId are not on the tape at all. I was also wrong about
  where the loss happens: I'd pointed at composite.rs's four state carriers, but
  they hold no trace data — by then there is nothing left to drop. The loss is
  upstream at the tag-emission boundary in emit.rs/tags.rs.

  There is a cheap path, and it needs one measurement before it's worth
  building.

● The strategic picture has sharpened, and it's worth stating before more code 
  gets written.

  Everything a filter can ever compute is a function of what actually reaches
  it: the surface form, the morpheme-id sequence, the root position, and static 
  grammar tables. That's the whole input. It rules out anything needing
  allomorph identity, spans, or partner structure without emitter surgery — and
  emitter surgery has a recorded history of a six-orders-of-magnitude
  overgeneration regression when done naively (emit.rs:20-23).

  Two classes remain expressible, and they have very different prospects:

  Morpheme co-occurrence — checkable from the id sequence alone. This class was
  already measured and NO-GO'd on 2026-07-17: validity-gate rejections were ~3%
  of failing-candidate time. Today's census doesn't overturn that; the oracle's
  93.4% on Sena is not restricted to co-occurrence.

  Surface consistency — "does any assignment of allomorphs and rules exist that
  could produce this surface from this morpheme sequence?" This needs no
  allomorph identity on the tape, because it quantifies existentially over the
  static grammar. It's unmeasured, and it's where slices 5–6 actually live.

  So the question that sizes this entire program is: what fraction of Sena's 
  93.4% oracle ceiling is reachable by a surface-consistency check? If most of
  it, the cheap path works with no tape change. If little, the program needs
  emitter surgery and its scope roughly triples.

  There's also a trap in the pass we've already built. structural.transition.v1
  is likely vacuous under ExploreMode::Pruned, the production default — Pruned
  already consults MorphotacticIndex before every recursive emission step, so
  the illegal transitions the pass would reject are never proposed. It can only
  earn its keep as a replacement for Pruned under Flat, which the plan
  anticipated. That makes the cheap fact-table's value contingent on axis 2
  rather than independent of it.

  I'm deliberately not dispatching the next agent yet. One is currently editing
  filter_ceiling_census.rs and will commit in the candidate-filter worktree; a
  second implementation agent there would race it on index.lock, which is a
  hazard I've already caused damage with once. It waits until that one lands.

  Running: the pruned-re-run census. Recorded for you: tasks #22 (generator, now
  scoped with the cheap/expensive split) and #24 (the circumfix finding,
  written up so nobody rebuilds it).

❯ give me a status and have sonnet subagents do the work, you review.           

● Status, then I'll hand the remaining work to a Sonnet agent.

  Where the filter program actually stands

  Measured today (oracle ceiling, per-word, three grammars):

  ┌────────────┬─────────────────────────┬───────────────┬──────────────────┐
  │  grammar   │  p99 confirm before →   │ filter cost   │     verdict      │
  │            │          after          │      p99      │                  │
  ├────────────┼─────────────────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ Sena       │ 354.6 → 24.6 ms         │ 0.678 ms      │ decisive win     │
  │            │ (−93.1%)                │               │                  │
  ├────────────┼─────────────────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ Amharic    │ 1508.5 → 1043.7 ms      │ 0.015 ms      │ partial          │
  │            │ (−30.8%)                │               │                  │
  ├────────────┼─────────────────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ Indonesian │ 2.744 → 2.744 ms (0.0%) │ 0.004 ms      │ free, wins       │
  │            │                         │               │ nothing          │
  └────────────┴─────────────────────────┴───────────────┴──────────────────┘

  Achieved saving is 0.00% on all three. Every TraceFact arrives Deferred, so no
  sound pass can fire. That — not pass count — is the blocker, so slices 5–7
  were re-gated onto a new task for the fact generator.

  Four findings that constrain the design:
  - The tape carries only morpheme ids + root position. Role, slot, stratum,
  spans, allomorph identity are not on it. Loss is at the tag-emission boundary
  in emit.rs/tags.rs.
  - Your circumfix example can't be built as modeled. A circumfix is one rule
  owning one morpheme producing one chain entry; the halves never exist
  separately. PartnerOpen/PartnerClose needs two units. Requires a MorphRuleDef
  change — and that also blocks the circumfix half of axis 2, leaving
  ExploreMode::Flat as the only instance standing.
  - steps is not comparable across grammars (~570× spread in ms/step), and for
  Amharic it overstates the win.
  - On Sena, once filtering works the proposer becomes the bottleneck (propose
  p99 24.9 ms vs filtered confirm 24.6 ms), which is direct evidence for your
  axis-2 thesis.

  Two corrections to my own earlier claims: the "after" column was a model, not
  a measurement; and I'd wrongly blamed composite.rs for the information loss.

  Also done and independently verified by me: the find-scope hook (379093d6) —
  denies chained, subshell, and piped root scans; one disclosed gap (find -H /).

  Why I was doing implementation myself: three subagents died on API 529s
  mid-task. I reviewed the survivor's 300-line diff, found two real defects (a
  mislabeled "order" control where the pruned run was never actually first, and
  a modelled column mixing timings across two different runs), and rewrote it to
  three calls per word. Handing it back off now.

● Agent(Finish and run the census A/B) Sonnet 5
  ⎿  Backgrounded agent (↓ to manage · ctrl+o to expand)

● A Sonnet agent now owns finishing the census — compile, smoke test, run all
  three grammars, hygiene, commit — and reports the three-column table back to
  me for review.

  Queued behind it, in the order I'd run them (one writer per worktree, so these
  go sequentially):

  1. Size the reachable fraction of the ceiling. The question that decides the
  program: what share of Sena's 93.4% can a surface-consistency check reach —
  "does any allomorph/rule assignment exist that could produce this surface from
  this morpheme sequence?" That's the strongest check expressible from morpheme
  ids plus static grammar. The other expressible class, co-occurrence, was
  already NO-GO'd at ~3% of failing time. If surface consistency reaches most of
  the ceiling, the cheap path works with no tape change; if not, this needs
  emitter surgery and the scope roughly triples.
  2. Task 16 — evidence-layer defects. Three verified vacuous assertions,
  including one where deleting a branch lets a MalformedIdentity proof verify
  against a legitimate rootless candidate. These undermine the evidence the rest
  rests on.
  3. Task 23 — Mbugwe and Aweti, which contribute nothing today and are the two
  most differently-shaped grammars.

  One risk worth flagging on the run in flight: the census now does three
  confirm calls per word instead of one. Sena's previous 1000-word pass took ~51
  s of census time on top of a long lexc compile, so it may exceed the
  foreground limit and need backgrounding. That's a scheduling nuisance, not a
  threat to the numbers — but if it times out mid-corpus, the result is censored
  and I won't treat a partial run as certifying anything.

  Worth recording, since this is the second time today the same trap fired: an
  agent told to "check first, don't launch a duplicate build" correctly avoided

  covers exactly this ("Never let an agent poll a background job it spawned"),
  and I'll include both halves in future dispatches.

● Verified independently, and it overturns my morning conclusion.

  My own Indonesian rerun, on the deterministic counter rather than wall time:

  ┌─────────────────────┬────────┬────────────────┬────────────────┐
  │     Indonesian      │ before │ after-modelled │ after-measured │
  ├─────────────────────┼────────┼────────────────┼────────────────┤
  │ steps/word p99      │ 33     │ 33             │ 5              │
  ├─────────────────────┼────────┼────────────────┼────────────────┤
  │ steps/word max      │ 158    │ 104            │ 16             │
  ├─────────────────────┼────────┼────────────────┼────────────────┤
  │ confirm ms/word p99 │ 2.115  │ 2.115          │ 1.479          │
  └─────────────────────┴────────┴────────────────┴────────────────┘

  Indonesian is not a null result. The model said p99 steps go 33→33 (0.0%); the
  real pruned re-run gives 33→5. My "0.0% at p99, structural and permanent"
  claim this morning was wrong, and wrong for exactly the reason I flagged as a
  hypothesis: the whole-chunk-only accounting rule understated it. One word
  makes it concrete — mengamat-amati runs 8.0 ms cold, 8.28 ms on the warm
  repeat (so no cache benefit at all), and 2.84 ms pruned.

  All three grammars move the same way — measured beats modelled everywhere, so
  this morning's ceilings were floors:

  ┌────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  grammar   │     p99 confirm: before → modelled → measured     │
  ├────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Sena       │ 348.0 → 24.3 → 15.6 ms                            │
  ├────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Amharic    │ 1446.3 → 985.1 → 651.5 ms (−30.8% becomes −55.0%) │
  ├────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Indonesian │ 2.115 → 2.115 → 1.479 ms                          │
  └────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  The fusion-break risk I raised did not materialize — the opposite happened. No
  grammar printed the FUSION BROKE line. Sena's chunks go 8605 → 981, but it
  had 1343 surviving chunks, so pruning re-fused surviving candidates into ~360
  fewer parse calls than even the survivors needed separately. That's why
  measured beats modelled: pruning doesn't just narrow chunks, it changes which
  chunks exist.

  That also refines the earlier code investigation. Its mechanism was right — a
  chunk's cost is fixed by root_key/union_rules before membership re-enters —
  but its conclusion ("bounded by RULE_UNION_SLACK, so small") was wrong,
  because re-grouping operates on which chunks form at all, not just how wide
  their unions get.

  The warm-repeat control never fired WARMTH DOMINATES: warmth alone moves
  Indonesian's p99 −3.4% against pruning's −30.1%, Sena's −1.9% against −95.5%.
  No STEPS NONDETERMINISM anywhere, and timed_out = 0 on all three runs.

  One honest caveat on precision: the agent got Indonesian p99 −11.6% where my
  rerun got −30.1%. At n=117, p99 is essentially the second-worst word, so
  wall-time percentiles there are noisy. The deterministic steps column is the
  number to trust within a grammar — and it moved 33→5.

  Committed as 918f8642, hygiene clean, one violation fixed. Next I'll fold
  these into the plan doc, then dispatch the surface-consistency measurement
  that sizes how much of this now-larger ceiling is actually reachable.

  Meanwhile, what's worth doing next. Five candidates, and one new lead the
  measurement just handed us:

  My recommendation: size the reachable fraction first, before building 
  anything. We now know the ceiling is large and that the filter's entire input
  is (surface, morpheme ids, root index, static grammar). Two check classes are
  expressible from that; one — co-occurrence — was already refuted at ~3% of
  failing time. So the whole program rests on whether surface consistency ("does
  any allomorph/rule assignment exist that could produce this surface from this
  morpheme sequence?") reaches most of Sena's ceiling. That's a measurement,
  not a build, and it decides whether the next step is a cheap lookup table or
  emitter surgery with a six-orders-of-magnitude regression history. Building
  the generator before knowing this risks building the wrong thing well.

  Then, conditional on that: the generator's cheap path — a static table keyed
  on MorphemeId giving Known(TraceRole), plus Known slot/stratum for rules
  referenced at exactly one site. No tape change. This is what finally moves
  achieved saving off 0.00%.

  Independent of both, and worth running in parallel: task 16's evidence-layer
  defects. Three verified vacuous assertions, including one where deleting a
  branch lets a MalformedIdentity proof verify against a legitimate rootless
  candidate. These sit under everything else we'll measure.

  Then: Mbugwe and Aweti, which contribute nothing today and are the two most
  differently-shaped grammars — so today's "1 of 3 decisive" claim rests on a
  sample missing exactly the cases most likely to break it. And
  ExploreMode::Flat, now the stronger axis for Sena and the only axis-2 instance
  still standing after circumfix died.

  The new lead, honestly caveated: pruning collapsed Sena's 1343 surviving
  chunks into 981 actual parse calls. My first instinct was that this exposes
  ~27% of parse calls as waste recoverable without any filter — but that's wrong
  on reflection: the re-fusion is caused by removing the doomed candidates,
  since they force their own grouping while present. What it does give us is
  real data for the RULE_UNION_SLACK tradeoff, which currently sits at 3 with a
  documented hazard (a broadened union made kutongera 3–5× slower). Worth a
  look, not a free win.

  Tell me if you'd rather go straight at the generator — the argument for it is
  that a working filter, even a weak one, converts 0.00% into a real number and
  makes everything downstream concrete.

● Two Sonnet agents are running in dedicated worktrees off 918f8642, and the
  record is committed.

  Agent 1 — the generator (filter-generator): builds the static grammar-level
  fact table that finally emits Known facts, with the soundness rule that a fact
  is only Known when it's unambiguous — role only if every allomorph of a
  morpheme classifies the same, slot/stratum only for rules referenced at
  exactly one site. Multi-sited stays Deferred. It also builds the gate that
  matters most: assert the filter's rejected set is a subset of what HC rejects.
  Discarding a candidate HC would accept is the one unacceptable failure, and I
  told it that if the subset check fails, that's the headline finding and not
  something to tune around. I also warned it that StructuralTransitionPass may
  be vacuous under ExploreMode::Pruned — and that reporting zero rejections
  honestly beats manufacturing them by weakening soundness.

  Agent 2 — reachability (filter-reach): measures what share of the removable
  work each expressible check class can actually reach, weighted by confirmation
  cost rather than candidate count, since the whole lesson so far is that
  killable candidates aren't deletable work.

  The plan doc now records the correction properly (5f3332b3): today's ceilings
  were floors, Indonesian was never a null result, and the mechanism is
  re-grouping rather than within-chunk narrowing — Sena's 1343 surviving chunks
  collapse to 981 parse calls because pruning changes which chunks exist, which
  the slack bound doesn't cap.

  Rust filter code. Once it's green: candidate-filter-first fast-forwards, then
  tooling-disk-recovery rebases onto the new tip and re-runs its (cheap,

● Soundness shape is right: Known only on unanimity, out-of-range defers rather
  than panics, unowned morphemes stay Deferred, and unmapped roles become
  distinct opaque Other(n) ids so no pass can mistake a circumfix for a prefix.
  The load-bearing piece is unique_slot_of — if that returns a slot when a rule
  actually has several sites, the whole safety argument collapses.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Two things to verify — and one is a potential soundness hole. rule_sites is
  built with filter_map(|site| sites.get(site).copied()), which silently drops
  any site missing from sites. If a rule has two sites and one is dropped, the
  vec has length 1 and unique_slot_of returns Some — asserting a unique slot for
  a multi-sited rule. That would break recall exactly where it's hardest to
  notice.

  Read 1 file, ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● allomorphs_of already exists (emit.rs:517, pub(crate)), so no emitter change
  was needed — good.

  The soundness hole is real. sites skips any template index past u16 or slot
  index past u8 (continue at lines 117 and 123), so a template with more than
  255 slots would have its later slots absent from sites. A rule sited at both
  slot 3 and slot 300 would then have its list silently shrunk to one entry and
  be reported as uniquely sited. Narrow, but it's the exact failure that
  discards valid parses, and our standing rule is to design for full-scale
  grammars rather than justify by what reference grammars happen to contain.
  Fixing it to fail closed:

● Both agents resumed with their context intact — back to them doing the work,

  What my review of the generator found while they were out. The design is
  sound: produce.rs is purely additive and leaves legacy untouched, facts.rs
  marks a fact Known only on unanimity across allomorphs, unowned morphemes stay
  Deferred, and unmapped roles become distinct opaque Other(n) ids so no pass
  can mistake a circumfix for a prefix. It also uses the real FST proposer,
  which quietly closes task 19.

  One real soundness hole, which I fixed. FilterIndex::build translated a rule's
  sites with filter_map(|site| sites.get(site).copied()), and sites skips
  template indices past u16 and slot indices past u8. A rule sited at both slot
  3 and slot 300 of one template would have had its list silently shrunk to a
  single entry, and unique_slot_of would then have declared it uniquely sited —
  an unsound Known slot fact. That's precisely the failure that discards a valid
  parse, and it would surface as silently missing analyses rather than an
  error. It now forfeits the whole rule when any site fails to translate. Narrow
  in practice, but "no reference grammar has 256 slots" is not an argument we
  accept here.

  I've flagged that fix to the agent as behaviour to keep, and told it I have
  not compiled the edit — it needs to verify, not assume.

  The step I'm holding it to before I believe anything: break the recall gate 
  and watch it fail. It died mid-way through exactly that check. Until a gate
  has been seen to fail, it has proven nothing, and this is the one gate
  standing between us and silently dropping valid parses.

  Still open for you, unchanged: the merge to main is green on both branches but
  blocked by the uncommitted Mbugwe budget work in the main checkout (backed up
  to a patch, untouched).

  But two things change the conclusion, and both matter.

  First, "co-occurrence is dead" overreaches in exactly the way this repo 
  forbids. The finding is that all three grammars declare no such rules, so the
  check is vacuous on them. That is not the same as the check being worthless —
  it's unmeasured on any grammar that does declare them. Our standing rule is
  that "zero uses in reference grammars" is never a justification, and the
  2026-07-17 NO-GO inherits the same limitation since it was measured on these
  same corpora. The honest statement: vacuous on our sample, value unknown in
  general.

  accounting we disproved this morning. They use shadow::chunk_is_removable —

  16–20% Sena) is a floor, not a verdict, and the recommendation not to expect
  (b) to help Sena's tail rests on the conservative model rather than a
  measurement. The ratio may hold since numerator and denominator share the
  model — but Indonesian is the proof that it can fail badly.

❯ Are we considering any filters currently that would make FST generation       
  simpler and smaller?                                                          

● Short answer: one, and it's running right now. Let me be precise about what's
  actually in play versus what I'd be speculating about.

  Live: ExploreMode::Flat replacing ExploreMode::Pruned — agent A is measuring
  it as we speak. This is the real shrinkage lever: Flat skips the morphotactic
  automaton entirely. If the Transition pass absorbs what Flat lets through, the
  proposer stops needing to build and consult that pruning machinery at all.
  Today's generator work is what made this testable — before GrammarFacts, the
  pass couldn't decide anything, so there was nothing to absorb with.

  Dead: circumfix N×M → N+M. That was the plan's other concrete shrinkage
  instance, and it needs the halves to be separately representable, which
  requires a MorphRuleDef change. Blocked, not cheap.

  Explicitly not a shrinkage lever: surface consistency. It's a post-proposal
  check — it removes nothing from the network. Worth stating plainly so it
  doesn't get credited on this axis.

  So the honest count is one live instance out of two, which is thin. Let me
  check one candidate I noticed during the emission investigation but haven't
  verified.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● That check settles a question rather than opening one, and in a useful
  direction.

  The FST already declines to encode allomorph environments. precision.rs
  documents that v1's emitter "emits every allomorph permissively regardless of
  its declared RequiredEnvironments/ExcludedEnvironments and lets HC confirm
  prune the wrong-environment candidates," with Strip as the permanent default.
  So the "propose sloppily, let something downstream sort it out" strategy is
  already fully exploited on the environment axis — there's no precision
  machinery sitting in the network waiting to be traded away for a filter. A
  filter that checked environments would offload work from HC, but it would not
  remove a single state from the FST.

  That means FST size is now structural, and only three things make it big:

  ┌──────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────┐
  │         structural cost          │      can a filter take it over?      │
  ├──────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Morphotactic pruning (Pruned)    │ Yes — being measured now (Flat +     │
  │                                  │ Transition pass)                     │
  ├──────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Circumfix N×M allomorph          │ Yes in principle — blocked on a      │
  │ cross-product                    │ MorphRuleDef change                  │
  ├──────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Per-allomorph lexc entries,      │ No — the network needs surface forms │
  │ per-group slot chains            │  to match input at all               │
  └──────────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────┘

  So the honest answer to your question is: one live candidate, one blocked, and
  the third isn't a candidate. That's thinner than I'd like, and the circumfix
  one looks more important than I treated it this morning — it's the only
  structural cross-product we've identified where a check could replace
  materialization, and it's sitting behind a data-model change we scoped out of
  this project.

  Why this matters more than it did yesterday: on Sena, once confirmation is
  filtered, the proposer becomes the larger half (propose p99 24.9 ms vs
  filtered confirm 24.6 ms; mean 15.4 vs 2.2). Sena's confirm-side headroom is
  nearly exhausted, so further wins there have to come from the proposer. That
  makes shrinkage the higher-value axis for exactly the grammar with the worst
  tail — and it's the axis with the fewest live options.

  If Flat pays off, that's the answer for Sena. If it doesn't, I'd argue the
  circumfix cross-product stops being out-of-scope and becomes the next thing
  worth costing out, because we'd have run out of cheap structural levers.

● This pass has a soundness risk I want to test before anything else, and it's
  testable with a specific word.

  The check rejects when no allomorph combination's literal characters fit
  inside the surface's character multiset. That's only sound if phonological
  rules never delete or substitute segments — and deletion is exactly what
  Indonesian nasal substitution does. meN- + pukul → memukul, where the root's p
  disappears. If the grammar models that with a phonological rule rather than a
  root allomorph, the check will demand a p the surface doesn't have and reject
  a candidate HC confirms.

  From this morning's census, memukul had 1 candidate and 0 empty buckets — HC
  accepted it. So this is a concrete, falsifiable prediction of a recall
  violation.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Confirmed the hazard: pukul has exactly one allomorph, PhoneticShape = 
  "pukul". No ukul variant.

  So the p in memukul is deleted by a phonological rule, not selected away by an
  allomorph. The check will therefore demand a p that the surface doesn't
  contain, find no fitting combination, and reject — while HC confirms that
  candidate.

  The pass is unsound as written, and the reason is structural rather than a
  patchable special case: character-multiset containment is only valid if
  phonology never deletes or substitutes segments, and deletion is precisely
  what these rules do. The membaca bug the agent found and patched was the same
  failure surfacing through archiphoneme notation; excluding notation characters
  fixed one symptom, not the premise.

● I found a recall violation in the surface-consistency pass before it could be 
  reported as working.

  The check rejects when no allomorph combination's literal characters fit
  inside the surface word's character multiset. That premise holds only if
  phonological rules never delete or substitute segments — and deletion is
  precisely what they do.

  The falsifying case, verified in the grammar rather than argued: Indonesian
  pukul has exactly one allomorph, PhoneticShape = "pukul", with no ukul
  variant. Nasal substitution deletes the root-initial stop, so meN- + pukul →
  memukul with no p. The check demands a p against {m:2, e:1, u:2, k:1, l:1},
  finds nothing that fits, and rejects. This morning's census shows memukul with
  1 candidate and 0 empty buckets — HC accepts it.

  That's discarding a valid parse: the one failure this design forbids, and one
  that surfaces in production as silently missing analyses rather than an error.

  Why the existing evidence couldn't have caught it. The membaca bug the agent
  found and patched was the same failure showing through archiphoneme notation —
  excluding notation characters fixed a symptom, not the premise. And the 80.7%
  / 77.4% Amharic reach figures were computed over the doomed set only, so they
  never tested false positives against the accepted set. Reach means nothing
  until soundness holds.

  I've told the agent to make the failure real by running its gate on Indonesian
  rather than take my word for it, to commit the work marked unsound rather
  than lose it, and explicitly not to rescue it by excluding more characters —
  that path already misled us once, and it makes the check detect less while
  leaving the premise false.

  The question I've put to it may well answer "no": whether any sound version
  exists, given that a sound variant would have to count only characters no
  phonological rule can touch — which means analysing the phonology, not reading
  allomorph text. If that isn't cheap, then "the only remaining cheap check is
  unsound" is the honest result, and better than a filter that silently drops
  parses.

● The only remaining cheap check is unsound, and I can now show it from the 
  grammar rather than argue it.

  Of the Indonesian grammar's five phonological rules, four are deletions —
  rules with no output segments. Deletion isn't an exotic case there; it's the
  bulk of the phonology. Character-multiset containment assumes a morpheme's
  literal characters survive into the surface, and in this grammar they
  routinely don't.

  The falsifying case is concrete and verified in the source data: pukul has
  exactly one allomorph with no ukul variant, nasal substitution deletes the
  root-initial stop, so memukul contains no p. The check demands a p, finds
  nothing that fits, and rejects a candidate HC accepts (1 candidate, 0 empty
  buckets in this morning's census).

  This also invalidates the reach figures as evidence. The 80.7% / 77.4% Amharic
  numbers were computed over the doomed set only, so by construction they could
  never detect a false positive against the accepted set. They measured how
  much the check catches, never what it wrongly catches.

  So the scoreboard for producing a nonzero rejection is now:

  ┌────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │            path            │                   status                   │
  ├────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ OwnershipPass              │ vacuous — pins_resolve short-circuits      │
  ├────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ StructuralTransitionPass   │ vacuous — Pruned pre-empts it              │
  ├────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ ExploreMode::Flat          │ refuted — expands the network, identical   │
  │                            │ candidates                                 │
  ├────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Morpheme co-occurrence     │ vacuous on all three grammars (none        │
  │                            │ declared)                                  │
  ├────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Surface consistency        │ unsound — discards valid parses            │
  │ (naive)                    │                                            │
  └────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  A sound variant of the last one exists in principle: exclude every character
  some phonological rule could delete or alter. That means analysing rule
  targets — natural classes, alpha variables — rather than reading allomorph
  text, and it yields a strictly weaker check. Whether enough signal survives to
  be worth anything is the open question, and I've put it to the agent rather
  than assumed the answer.

  I've recorded this in durable memory, because the previous note listed surface
  consistency as the promising path and that would have misled the next
  session.

  Sena has no phonological rewriting stage and no modifying morphological
  action, so surface material is root text plus inserted affix text and
  character containment is genuinely sound there. Sena is also the grammar with
  the largest ceiling (93.6% of steps), the worst tail (p99 348 ms), and 20.2%
  of removable work within surface-consistency's reach.

  So the fix isn't to weaken the check — it's to gate it on a proven 
  precondition of the grammar, the same capability-check shape this repo already
  uses. The pass may reject only where the grammar provably cannot delete or
  alter material, and defers everywhere else, which is always safe.

  I deliberately did not let the agent implement that precondition as "no
  PhonologicalRule element" — that's necessary but not sufficient, and I'd be
  handing it my own unverified list. It has to establish sufficiency from the
  model, enumerating the action and rule enums exhaustively so a new variant is
  a compile error rather than a silent "safe". I specifically flagged
  truncation: this repo documents 41 zero-width truncation mrules in Aweti, and
  a subrule whose output fails to copy every input part deletes material exactly
  like a phonological rule does. Sena's 239 subrules each need checking.

  The deliverable I asked for proves both directions: Indonesian's precondition
  must fail with memukul surviving, and Sena's must pass with a non-empty
  rejected set — which would be the first non-vacuous run of the recall gate in
  this project, and the first nonzero achieved saving.

● Back to the agent for execution. Two things I did while it was
  session-limited, both flagged to it for independent verification rather than
  trust:

  Its deliberate soundness break was still live in the tree. It died
  mid-verification with grammar_preserves_segments forced to true || (...). Had
  that been committed and run, the pass would have certified every grammar and
  rejected valid parses on Indonesian — the precise failure the precondition
  exists to prevent. Reverted, and I've asked it to grep the worktree for any
  other survivor.

  The precondition as written would have disqualified Sena and produced another 
  null result. Measuring the segment definitions showed why:

  ┌────────────┬─────────────────────┬───────────┐
  │  grammar   │ single-rep segments │ multi-rep │
  ├────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ Sena       │ 39                  │ 1 (m/n)   │
  ├────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ Indonesian │ 28                  │ 1 (g/G)   │
  ├────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ Amharic    │ 376                 │ 41        │
  └────────────┴─────────────────────┴───────────┘

  A grammar-level disqualifier throws Sena away over one segment in forty — and
  Sena is the grammar with the 93.6% ceiling and the 348 ms tail. So I made it
  per-character: multi-representation segments are never required, rather than
  fatal to the whole grammar. Requiring fewer characters can lose a rejection
  but cannot manufacture a false one. I asked the agent to check that argument
  and say if it disagrees, since its own kakamwe finding is what motivated it.

  Worth noting what the agent found that I'd missed: Indonesian's memukul
  deletion is a morphological action, not a phonological rule. My mechanism was
  wrong even though the conclusion held — "no PhonologicalRule" would have
  certified Indonesian and rejected a valid parse. It also discovered the
  multi-spelling hazard empirically, via its own recall gate on kakamwe, rather
  than by reasoning ahead.

  The deliverable is now sharp: Indonesian and Amharic preconditions must fail,
  Sena's must pass with a non-empty rejected set. That would be the first
  non-vacuous run of the recall gate and the first nonzero achieved saving in
  this project.
